+3.8 sd), which is the control the selected-on-the-same-data five require. ⚠ **What is withdrawn: the sign agreement itself carries almost no information.** 16 of the 20 variables are same-signed, so a random 5-subset agrees **28.2%** of the time. ⚠ **`#369`: and the sharing is uniform along that line — the two numbers above are not in tension.** A quintile split had no power to say so (MDE +0.20 against a meaningful +0.05, `#368`), so the question was re-estimated as a single interaction on all 6,609 people: `c3⁻ × PC1` = **−0.00042** (se 0.0120, 95% CI [−0.024, +0.023 [R15 3× curveball · scope stated]]), **−0.04 sd** from an offset null built by permuting **only** PC1 — the null is not zero because the product term is collinear with both main effects (corr(c3⁻, PC1) = +0.1632). **MDE 0.030 for linear moderation and 0.150 for a step confined to the top quintile**, and the quintile bound already excludes steps ≥0.20, so the two shapes leave no gap. **Weak covariance and strong sharing coexist: sharing needs the direction to be right, not the magnitude to be large.** In plain terms — this line sets how much shame a person carries, not whether their taste will shame them. ⚠ For scale, not as a test (k differs): a variable that genuinely **is** c3⁻ plus equal-magnitude noise leaves 54.8%. ⚠ c3⁻ is estimated in-sample (`#347`), so the absolute pp is an upper bound; the retained fraction is the transferable quantity. |
| **rho_i** — whether your rare interests come first or last | **+0.5316** | onset ages (31 categories) | n/a (blocks carry no time) | `UNCOMPUTED` | 11/31 | current age controlled (+0.0226) · recall bias controlled (`#114` — **`#452` corrects this citation, which read `#289`; that entry is about measurement invariance by sex**) · 56% survives a cross-half split |
| **`form_i` — what "opened by drawn things" and "opened by written things" share** | **+0.3382** (two items) | blocks (the residual of `animated`/`written` after all six coordinates) | **verified** — +0.0799 on the disjoint onset instrument, 3.9× its permutation spread (`#277`) | **+0.0454**, but its increment over the six is **+0.21 ± 0.13pp — indistinguishable** (`#278`) | **9–11 of 27** (its own two items excluded) | the six coordinates are removed by construction; its correlation with `c3⊥D` is an **identity**, not evidence (`#277`); it shares only +0.1212 of profile with `rate⊥rho` on the disjoint instrument (`#283`) |
| ~~Accumulation rate~~ | +0.6931 | onset ages | n/a | `UNCOMPUTED` | 6/31 | **withdrawn as a dimension** — its profile sits at the ceiling against rho_i (−0.8499 vs 0.8779) |

**Read the reliability column first.** The three breadth types (0.56–0.72) are the most reliable person-level
quantities in this release, and they were the last to be found. The position score (0.432), which carries the
project's oldest and most-cited claim, is the least reliable of the ones still standing. `[#251]`
---

> ⚠ **This table is built on the pooled sample, and the six coordinates are not the same thing in the two ⚠ **`#288`: dropping every item that mentions male/female/gender halves this but does not remove it** — on the 24 non-gender-referential outcomes the gap is **−0.1229**, and on the 22 that remain after excluding the two cells already seen it is **−0.1172, still 3.0× its own spread**. **Roughly half the non-invariance comes from a few gendered items; the other half does not.** ⚠ The gender-referential subset itself is only 5 outcomes — too few for a correlation, so it is `UNCOMPUTED`, not zero, and the "half" is a subtraction (`D5`), not a measurement. ⚠ **`#293`–`#294`: the two groups do not even agree on what counts as uncommon.** The rarity vector `rar = −log(option prevalence)` is computed once on the pooled sample and applied to everyone; median within-block prevalence correlation is **+0.9039** across the sex split against **+0.9973 ± 0.0003** across random splits of the same sizes (**148.6× the spread**). ⚠ **But giving each group its own ruler does not remove the non-invariance**: rebuilt with within-group rarity, the gap goes from −0.2061 to **−0.2270** (**110.2% retained**), with a `SEX × S` interaction sweep flat at g=0. **Both facts are true and neither causes the other** — the difference is not in the ruler, it is in the thing being measured. ⚠ **`#296`: the outcome side is not yet controlled.** A group difference in the joint-R² curve can also come from the outcomes themselves (range restriction, response style), and partialling a response-style reference curve leaves **84.3%** of the gap — but that arm's positive control **fails** (planting noise in six outcomes for one group, the partialling makes the artificial gap **larger**, recovery **−18.5%**), so it is UNVERIFIED, not a confirmation. **The sex non-invariance is reported here at D5, not D6. ⚠ **`#381`: and it cannot be localised to the position score S — not because it isn't there, but because this dataset cannot ask.** With shame rank-transformed within each group, S's slope difference is +0.033 (|t| 1.16) against a family-wise threshold of 2.38 — but in the same model and the same run, **`EARLY`, which `#380` established as genuinely different, reaches only |t| 1.73 and also fails.** An instrument that cannot detect a difference it is known to have says nothing by its silence, so this is UNVERIFIED. **A synthetic positive control planted at 0.25 passes the same test at |t| 6.82** — it would have licensed the claim that S shows no sex difference, and the real-data reference is what stopped it. **Settling S would need the wide caliber's power, and S's definition needs ≥8 blocks; the two requirements exclude each other.** So this is a named wall, not an undone task. ⚠ **`#414`: and there is a second wall, of a different kind — the category level tops out at n = 31.** The onset instrument has exactly 31 categories, so any category-level correlation is estimated on 31 points: the offset null for such a correlation already has an sd of **0.146** and a 95th percentile of **0.292**, giving an MDE of about **0.45**. **Nothing smaller than that is visible at the category level here, and no amount of data adds categories.** The two walls differ in kind: S's sex difference needs power the wide caliber cannot supply because S itself requires ≥8 blocks; the category level needs more categories, and there are none. ⚠ A worked illustration of what that costs: ordering the 31 categories by how much earlier or later they arrive than their rarity predicts, the two latest have mean shame 0.591 and 0.756 against 0.370 and 0.467 for the two earliest — which reads like a clean positive relation while the actual correlation across all 31 is **+0.074**. **The two ends of a 31-point ordering are its noise, not its shape.**** ⚠ **`#298`–`#299`: the two readings cannot be separated on this data.** Rarity recomputed inside each person's own reference group (sex × age) separates content stigma from deviation-from-people-like-me — but only where the two rarity definitions disagree, and there they behave the same. Over the option-subset grid (keep the most reference-group-divergent 10/25/50/100% per block) the two definitions decorrelate from **+0.8865 to +0.6722**, and both routes **strengthen together** (+41.6% and +62% among people readable both ways) while their difference stays at **+0.0113 against a detectable +0.0918**, with the sign flipping in one of four cells. **The earlier reading that only content carries shame held on the full option set, where the two definitions are 90% the same quantity — it does not survive the grid.** ⚠ **`#304`: and the two routes are not equally solid objects.** Split the respondents in half twelve times and estimate each direction independently: `c3` points at itself across halves at **|cos| = 0.796 ± 0.103** (worst split 0.592) against a random-direction floor of **0.139** — a real direction, but a soft one. `c1` reaches 0.957, the three-dimensional subspace 0.852, and the structure behind **S reproduces at 0.997**. Changing the option-split seed moves c3's figure only 0.039. ⚠ Half-sample |cos| **understates** the stability of the released n=6,717 estimate. ⚠ **`#308`: and in a single person the two routes ADD.** Split at the medians, mean shame runs **0.383** (low on both) · **0.700** (unusual taste only) · **0.718** (transgressive spending only) · **1.077** (both) against an additive prediction of **1.034** — someone high on both carries **2.81×** the low/low group, which is exactly the sum. The continuous interaction is **−0.0150** against a permutation null of +0.0051 ± 0.0135, and the same test recovers a planted interaction of 0.15 as **+0.1502**. It survives controlling pick-count and splitting by sex (−0.024 … −0.008). **This is a null with power, not an absence of evidence.** ⚠ ** The interaction null carries its own resolution: **MDE = 0.038** against a meaningful interaction of ~0.05 (half a main effect). `#311`: and nothing buffers it.** Age, openness, a non-monogamous relationship style and a sexually liberated upbringing — a joint test over all eight (route × moderator) interactions gives an R² drop of **0.094pp** against a permutation null of **0.121 ± 0.061pp**, with **58% of permutations exceeding the observed value**. The same test detects a planted buffer of **10%** in three of the four variables (30% for openness), against **54.8%** for the single-coefficient test it replaces. **This is a null with power**: if any of the four took even a tenth off the shame coefficient, this design would have seen it. ⚠ **`#384`: and what shame changes is what a person has done, not what they believe about themselves.** Two items split this cleanly: whether they think they could stop being aroused by it (*Impossible* … *with little effort, yes*) and whether they have acted on everything that arouses them. Controlling S, c3⁻ and category count, shame predicts **−0.0833** (se 0.0123) on having acted — **−6.85 sd** past its own permutation threshold of 0.0238 — while on believing it could be stopped it predicts **+0.0135** against a threshold of 0.0248, **a null with power** (MDE 0.03 against a meaningful 0.05, and the rank-transformed version agrees in sign at +0.0144). **Two pre-registered readings both die**: shame as mobilised control (which predicts a positive belief coefficient) and shame as resignation (which predicts a negative one). ⚠ Direction is not identified — “shame suppressed the acting” and “having acted left less to be ashamed of” both fit this correlation. ⚠ **`#385`: one candidate explanation can be removed, though — it is not manufactured by when the interests arrived.** Splitting respondents into onset quartiles (median 12.5 / 14.7 / 16.7 / 19.9 years), the shame→acted coefficient is **−0.099 / −0.064 / −0.049 / −0.123**: all four negative and all four past a within-layer permutation threshold of 0.046, with a range of 0.074 that does **not** clear the 0.090 threshold from random equal-size strata. **The relation holds at every onset age and at the same size.** ⚠ This is partial orientation, **not an instrument**: onset age correlates with shame directly (−0.102), so the exclusion restriction fails and nothing here is causal — one rival explanation is gone, that is all. ⚠ Stratifying on a shame-correlated variable narrows within-layer shame variance, so only signs and threshold-crossings are compared, not magnitudes against the −0.083 pooled figure. ⚠ **`#386`: and the rival mechanism leaves its own trace — but the trace belongs to experience, not to shame.** If having acted is what reduces shame, the route runs through discovering the interest is still there, so people who have acted more should believe more strongly that it *cannot* be stopped. It checks out: `acted → belief` is **−0.0387** (se 0.0122, rank-transformed −0.0399), **−2.97 sd** past a threshold of 0.0246 built by permuting acting within the analysis mask. **And shame itself predicts that belief not at all** (+0.0135, a null with power, `#384`). ⚠ Strength is not comfortable: the design's MDE is 0.05, exactly the meaningful magnitude and above the observed 0.039, so roughly a quarter of replications would miss it — direction reportable, magnitude not. ⚠ Still not causal: acting and belief are measured at the same time, so this says the predicted trace is present, not which came first. ⚠ **`#387`: and the obvious next test is empty, by algebra rather than by power.** Controlling for acting inside the shame→belief regression moves the shame coefficient by exactly **−0.003221**, which is exactly **−a×b** from the two paths already reported — the omitted-variable identity of standardised OLS, agreeing to six decimals. It could not have come out otherwise, so it carries no information about the world. **The distinction matters for what to do next: a low-power test can be rescued with more data, an algebraically empty one never can.** The direction of this chain is not identifiable from these measurements, and no sample size changes that. ⚠ **`#388`: what does predict that belief is having the ordinary things work for you too.** Scanning 112 numeric person-level variables against the belief residual (after removing acting, S, c3⁻, category count and shame), the largest is **+0.0888** against a max-of-112-noise-columns threshold of 0.0419, and the top hits form one family — dirty talk, ordinary sex, oral sex — whose mean scores **+0.1159** against a single-variable null of 0.0219. **Overall rarity S is already controlled**, so this is residual to how unusual the person's tastes are: ⚠ **`#392` REVERSES THE DIRECTION OF THIS ENTIRE NOTE — and of `#390` and `#391` with it.** These five items are the complete `RATING_NEG_FIB` set (the survey has exactly five), and their values run only over {0, −1, −2, −3, −5, −8}, never positive, with **−8 the mode** and 0 the rarest response. Anchored against two count variables whose direction is fixed by construction, the five-item mean correlates **−0.4502** with `Totalsexacts` and **−0.1866** with total fetish categories — same sign, with the duplicated cunnilingus item pair correlating +0.697 as an internal-consistency positive control and noise-versus-anchor at −0.003. **So −8 is the erotic end: a higher score means the conventional works LESS well.** The correlation of +0.1255 therefore reads: **the less ordinary things do for someone, the more they believe they could stop.** ⚠ And the construct is not “conventional content” either — at −0.45 with the count of endorsed sex acts, the score is substantially general sexual responsiveness inverted. The original reading is kept below rather than deleted, because what makes this worth recording is that four rounds of gates passed over an unchecked sign: ~~it is not that conventional people feel changeable, it is that people for whom the conventional **also** works do.~~ ⚠ The family was defined after seeing the top hits; the pre-registered quantity is the maximum, which clears on its own, and the family score is a description of those hits rather than an independent test. ⚠ **`#389`: and that description does not survive — redefined by the questionnaire's own block structure it changes sign.** Using the two blocks `#327` identified as c3's ordinary end (`sex acts`, `sensations`) instead of five hand-picked items, the correlation is **−0.0259** against a single-variable threshold of 0.0253 — opposite in sign to +0.1159, with the positive reference firing and the negative control silent. **The family reading is withdrawn**; the pre-registered maximum (+0.0888 against 0.0419) never depended on that selection and stands. ⚠ The two definitions are not the same construct — hand-picked items rate *how erotic* conventional acts are, the block version counts *how many* options were ticked and correlates +0.34 with total picks — but that is a limitation of the replication, not a rescue of the claim. ⚠ **`#395`: and the same score also predicts shame — it is the common antecedent of two things that are unrelated to each other.** With the direction now fixed, the five-item score predicts shame at **+0.0827** raw and **+0.0657** controlling S, c3⁻ and category count (se 0.0126), **+5.10 sd** past a permutation threshold of 0.0247. So the less ordinary things do for someone, the **more** ashamed they are — while the same score independently predicts believing they could stop, and shame and that belief are themselves unrelated (`#384`). ⚠ MDE 0.05 exactly equals the meaningful magnitude here, so the strength is not comfortable. ⚠ **`#396`: and a whole class of follow-up tests is closed — by algebra.** Adding a control M and reading where X's coefficient moves reproduces **−a×b** exactly (predicted −0.007123, observed −0.007123, agreeing to six decimals) whenever `X→M` and `M→Y|X` have both already been estimated. **It carries zero new information; it is a test only when one of the two legs is unestimated.** This is the second such case on this page, and the reason it keeps getting proposed is that it *reads* like a mediation analysis — but mediation's information comes from its assumption of no unmeasured confounding, not from the arithmetic. Strip the assumption and only the identity is left. ⚠ **`#397`: and the five-item score turns out to be a fourth shame route, separate from c3⁻.** The two correlate only **+0.2395**, and entered together they both survive: the five-item score goes +0.0881 → **+0.0657** (threshold 0.0235) and c3⁻ +0.1093 → **+0.0932** (threshold 0.0264), losing 25% and 15% respectively. **Having unusual tastes and finding that ordinary things do little for you are two different things, and each carries shame on its own** — someone with entirely conventional tastes can still be ashamed because the conventional does not move them either. ⚠ c3⁻ is an eigenvector whose sign is arbitrary and was anchored against shame before use (+0.127). ⚠ Notably this route arrived through a different door: it was found while scanning the residual of the *changeability* item, not while looking for shame routes. ⚠ **`#398`–`#399`: scanning a third outcome — whether engaging with one's arousal feels therapeutic — finds two more, and they are not one construct.** That item is not inverted shame: with shame, S, c3⁻ and category count removed, the residual still has structure (largest \|r\| **0.1187** against a max-of-112-noise threshold of 0.0442, 10 of 21 candidates over). Two of the hits are new: *if my partner is aroused by something I can be too* (**+0.1038**) and *I can be aroused by a partner doing what I like even knowing they are not* (**+0.0661**), each surviving with the other absent from the model and barely moving when both are entered. **But they correlate only +0.031 with each other** — near orthogonal, consistent with their near-opposite content (absorbing a partner's arousal versus not needing it). **So these are two separate things that each predict the same outcome, not two indicators of one.** ⚠ Scans are discovery, not test; both were replicated only within this dataset. ⚠ **`#401`: and shame and healing are not two ends of one axis — 1,790 people report both.** Their linear correlation is **+0.0005** across all 15,501 respondents: not negative, zero. Split at both medians, the both-high cell holds **11.55%** of people (**1,790**) against an offset null of **10.43% ± 0.18%** built by keeping both marginal distributions and breaking only the pairing — **+6.13 sd above**, so these people are not merely possible but slightly **more** common than chance. ⚠ Nothing is controlled in this comparison, deliberately: the question is whether such people exist, and controlling would turn a group of people into a residual. ⚠ Same-source variance would inflate the correlation toward a single axis, so the near-zero reading is conservative. ⚠ **`#402`: and they are not a random slice of the ashamed — they have a shape.** Comparing both-high (n = 815 once the block-coverage rule for coordinates applies) against shame-only (n = 1,193) across eight person-level quantities, four clear a family-wise max-\|t\| threshold of 2.752 built from random equal-size regroupings: the five-item score at **d = −0.31** (\|t\| 6.80), position S at **+0.24** (5.27), mean onset at **−0.16** (3.45) and profile clarity at +0.13 (2.84). **So the both-high group has rarer tastes AND finds ordinary things more erotic, and their interests arrived earlier.** ⚠ Strikingly, c3⁻ — the largest shame route on this page — does **not** separate them (\|t\| 1.19) **— but read `#415` below before taking that as evidence about c3⁻: both cells here are high on shame, so c3⁻ has no room to vary, and on the other cut it reaches 5.15** — what distinguishes them along *this* cut is not the content of what they want but that they also have the ordinary half. ⚠ Both cells sit on the high-shame side, so nothing here measures differences in shame itself. ⚠ **`#415`: the other cut — both-high (815) against therapeutic-only (1,046) — isolates the shame dimension instead, and it picks out a nearly disjoint set of quantities.** There, the largest are **`EARLY` (d −0.291, \|t\| 6.24)** and **`c3⁻` (+0.241, 5.15)**, with `D` (+0.169, 3.62) also clearing a family-wise threshold of 2.681 — while the five-item score, which dominated the first cut at 6.80, falls to **1.54**. **So the content of what someone wants (c3⁻) marks the shame dimension, and "the ordinary works for me too" marks the therapeutic one; only S and EARLY mark both.** ⚠ This also qualifies the sentence above: c3⁻ failing to separate in the first cut is not evidence that it is irrelevant to these people — both cells there were high on shame, so c3⁻ had no room to vary. **A null from one cut is a null along that cut's direction only.** ⚠ **`#416`: and with all four cells used, the two dimensions turn out to be additive.** The third cut (shame-only versus therapeutic-only) carries no new information at all — it is exactly cut 2 minus cut 1 by construction, verified to **2.8e−17** — so a real interaction test needs the fourth cell, the 2,566 people low on both, which this project had never used. Doing it: the largest interaction `(both − shame-only) − (therapeutic-only − both-low)` is `EARLY` at **\|t\| 2.15** against a family-wise threshold of **2.73** from relabelling the four cells at random, with everything else below 1.16, and an MDE of 0.20 sd against a meaningful 0.20. **No quantity belongs specifically to the both-high cell.** So the shape those 1,790 people have is the **sum** of the two dimensions' shapes and nothing more — consistent with the earlier finding that "both halves" is not a construct. ⚠ **`#417`–`#418`: and the fourth cell — the 1,662 people low on both — is not simply "fainter everywhere".** It is strongly distinctive: position S differs by **d = −0.31 (\|t\| 10.17)**, with the five-item score +0.17, c3⁻ −0.17, mean onset +0.16 and profile clarity −0.09 also clearing a family-wise threshold of 2.73. **But orienting all eight quantities toward "more engaged" using an anchor that is neither shame nor a reading of mine** — the count of endorsed sex acts, whose direction is fixed by being a count and which plays no part in defining the cells — **only 6 of 8 point the faint way, against an offset null of 3.96 ± 1.46 whose 95th percentile is exactly 6.** With an MDE of 0.05 sd against a meaningful 0.20, that is a null with power. **So being low on both is a specific position, not a general dimming**: tastes markedly more ordinary and interests arriving later, but not less of everything. ⚠ **`#403`: and "both halves" behaves as one thing rather than two.** Combined as `z(S) − z(five-item)`, the score separates the two cells at **d = +0.399, \|t\| = 8.76**, and as `min(z(S), −z(five-item))` at **+0.388, 8.54** — **both above the best single quantity's 6.80**, which was the pre-registered bar precisely because a combination of two already-significant terms is otherwise guaranteed to clear an ordinary threshold. Two constructions, same verdict. ⚠ This is still the same dataset and the same pair of cells, so it is not a replication, and "stronger together" does not establish a latent variable. ⚠ **`#404`: and it does not become one on testing.** Putting the combination alongside its two components in a model of the *therapeutic* item — an outcome that played no part in constructing it — the combination falls from **+0.124** to **+0.018** against a permutation threshold of 0.046. ⚠ The additive version of the combination cannot even be tested: `z(S) − z(five-item)` is an exact linear function of its parts, so that design matrix is rank-deficient (3 of 4) and the coefficient is unidentifiable — a check worth three lines before any compute. ⚠ And the collapse itself is **not publishable as a null**: the design's MDE is 0.080 against a meaningful 0.05, so **the honest position is that there is no evidence for a latent variable and this design cannot rule one out either** — which is a different claim from having ruled it out. ⚠ One non-null aside: on the therapeutic outcome the two halves are **not** equal partners (rarity +0.047, ordinary-also-works +0.123), whereas they were equal in separating the two cells. ⚠ **`#405`: and across four outcomes the division of labour is not stable — this one is a null with power.** Fitting each of shame, changeability, therapeutic and having-acted on the same two halves with the same controls, the coefficient pairs point in directions spread over **195°** (−36°, −126°, +69°, +40°). Their circular dispersion is **0.662** against an offset null of **0.551 ± 0.216** — *above* the null mean, nowhere near its lower 5th percentile of 0.136 — with the ratio metric agreeing and an MDE of 0.10 against a meaningful 0.20. **So "both halves" is a good description of that group and not a construct**: it works where it was found and does not recur as one thing elsewhere. ⚠ The four outcomes intercorrelate, which deflates the dispersion and therefore biases toward *stability* — the instability survives a bias running the other way. ⚠ **`#406`: and position S itself does not point one way — it is reversed on 8 of 29 outcomes.** Every claim about S on this page comes from the shame outcome; fitted across the project's whole 29-outcome set with the same controls, S's coefficient is positive in **21** and negative in **8**, a sign consistency of **0.724** against an offset null of **0.579 ± 0.059** (upper 95th 0.690) built by permuting S while keeping the outcomes' intercorrelation. **That clears the bar by +2.47 sd — marginally — and the intercorrelation of the outcomes inflates consistency, so the bias runs toward the conclusion.** ⚠ The MDE sweep saturates at every level, because a sign-count statistic has almost no dynamic range: this design's MDE is **not measurable**, which is not the same as small. **So statements of the form "people high on S are …" have to carry "on the shame outcome"** — among the reversals is whether a person believes they could stop. ⚠ **`#409`: and splitting S in two does not repair it — a null with power.** Using the transgressive/ordinary block split that `#327` derived from loadings rather than from reading, the two halves' sign consistencies are **0.621** (transgressive) and **0.759** (ordinary) against the combined S's 0.724; the smaller of the two sits at **−0.87 sd** below an offset null of **0.651 ± 0.035** built by splitting the blocks at random into groups of the same sizes. **So S is not a mixture of two directions** — the transgressive half is if anything the more scattered one — and the outcome-specific qualifier above stands for a reason that has now been tested rather than assumed. ⚠ MDE 40% against a meaningful 50%, sweep unsaturated. ⚠ **`#411`: and shame barely suppresses action at all in people who have never had a partner.** Stratifying the shame→acted relation by partner count (0 versus ≥1), controlling age, category count and — in the narrow arm — S and c3⁻, the coefficient is **−0.015** among the never-partnered and **−0.077** among the rest, a difference of **−0.062** past a random-restratification threshold of 0.042 at the wide caliber (n = 12,516, guard-25 growth +89.4%). All three computable cells carry the pre-registered negative sign; only the widest clears. ⚠ **This establishes that the gap is not noise, not that shame needs an audience**: people who never had a partner have neither an audience nor an opportunity, and having-acted is depressed by both — no sample size separates them. ⚠ Wide and narrow calibers are different populations, and the wide arm drops S and c3⁻ because they require ≥8 blocks. ⚠ **`#390`: put in one model, intensity survives and breadth collapses — the withdrawal is reversed by a test registered in advance.** With both terms present (they correlate −0.37, so the collinearity world does not apply), the rating-based intensity score holds at **+0.1255** (se 0.0131, threshold 0.0253 from permuting both columns together) while the block-count breadth score falls from −0.0430 alone to **−0.0009**. So `#389`'s −0.026 was breadth speaking for intensity. **The three outcomes and their predictions were written before the run**, and the kill fired for the one that reinstates the reading — a withdrawal that stood for exactly one round and was undone by a pre-registered test, not by reinterpretation. ⚠ What this does **not** settle is the selection itself: the five items are still ones chosen by looking at the scan, so `#388`'s scope stands and the defensible pre-registered quantity remains the maximum, +0.0888. ⚠ **`#391`: within those five, though, it behaves like one family rather than one or two items wearing a name.** Entered singly, all five carry the same sign and all five clear their own single-variable thresholds (+0.090, +0.085, +0.069, +0.064, +0.063 against thresholds of 0.024–0.026), and dropping any one item leaves **86–104%** of the five-item score — the largest single contribution is 14%, and two of the items slightly dilute rather than carry it. This rules out the `#325` pattern where two indicators of one construct load it very unequally. ⚠ It does not touch the selection question: proving the five cohere is not proving the five were picked fairly. ⚠ **`#327`: and the block loadings reproduce that split on the other instrument.** Bootstrapped over people (300 draws, 23 of 32 intervals excluding zero), c3's shame-ward end is **urine** (+0.270), **nonconsent scenarios** (+0.263), **transformations** (+0.254) and **abnormal body states** (+0.244); its other end is **sex acts** (−0.287) and **sensations** (−0.268). Changing the option-split seed leaves the loading vector at r ≥ **+0.975**. ⚠ **A loading ranking is a description, not a name** — four naming attempts have already died here (`#201` `#202` `#303` `#324`). ⚠ **`#330`: and it is not rarity wearing a costume.** Strip the three content-free block properties — mean prevalence, option count, prevalence dispersion — from c3's loading vector entirely, and its shame correlation holds (**+0.1169**, bootstrap [+0.086, +0.130], overlapping the reference arm's [+0.111, +0.138]) while the split on the **disjoint onset instrument gets sharper**: transgressive half **+0.174 → +0.208**, ordinary half **−0.121 → −0.058**. Those three properties were adding noise to the cross-instrument separation, not carrying it. ⚠ Three properties are the three I thought of; this is a lower-bound argument, not a proof. ⚠ **`#332`: and a third correlate — how early it arrived.** Mean onset age correlates with shame at **−0.1024** raw and **−0.0810** after controlling both how many categories a person reports and their position score S (79% retained, 2×MDE = 0.068); **28 of 31 categories are negative** individually. Category split-half reliability is **0.924**, giving a disattenuated **−0.1065** with a bootstrap 95% interval of **[−0.134, −0.082]**. It survives dropping everyone with a censored ("26yo+") onset (**−0.0684**, 83% of the sample) and three different numeric codings of the age bins (range 0.004). ⚠ Onset age is retrospective self-report — association, not cause. ⚠ **`#380`: and this route is about twice as strong in respondents who are not biologically male.** At the wide caliber (n = 12,720), with shame **rank-transformed within each group** — which removes not only mean and variance differences in scale use but the response-shape confound that left `#296` at D5 — onset predicts shame at **−0.133** in group 0 versus **−0.068** in group 1 (difference +0.065, |t| = **3.69** against a random-equal-split family-wise threshold of 2.19). **All three onset summaries agree**: mean (|t| 3.69), median (3.51) and earliest (2.62) all carry the same sign and all clear their own thresholds. ⚠ They are not three independent tests — they intercorrelate +0.95/+0.65/+0.55 — but the least correlated of them, *earliest onset*, still clears. ⚠ Rank transformation discards magnitude, so only sign and threshold-crossing are compared, not effect sizes. ⚠ This is the reduced model (no S, no c3⁻): the wide caliber cannot carry c3⁻, whose profile requires ≥8 blocks, so “wide × full model” is impossible rather than undone. ⚠ At the narrow caliber with the full model the same difference is +0.053, |t| 2.14 against a threshold of 2.42 — same sign, below the bar. ⚠ **`#370`: and within a single person, the rare things arrive LATER — far more than the shared schedule explains.** For each respondent reporting ≥8 onset categories (n = 6,473), the rank correlation between their own onset ages and category rarity averages **+0.2219** (median +0.2532; **77.1%** of people positive) against an offset null of **+0.0884 ± 0.0034** — **+38.9 sd**. That null is not zero and could not be: at population level, rarer categories genuinely start later (corr of category median onset with rarity **+0.3406**), so the null is built by replacing every onset with a draw from that same category's own distribution, preserving the schedule and breaking only the individual's deviation. **Observed is 2.5× the null: almost everyone starts from the ordinary, and the ordering is personal, not merely demographic.** ⚠ **`#371`: and the few for whom it ran the other way carry more shame.** Regressing shame on that within-person ordering, controlling category count and S, gives **−0.0287** (95% CI [−0.047, −0.011], −3.00 sd from a person-shuffle null) at the wide caliber (n = 11,535) and **−0.0327** ([−0.057, −0.009]) at the narrow one (n = 6,473) — same sign, both past threshold. **Negative means the people whose rare interests arrived EARLY, before the ordinary ones, are the more ashamed.** The wide arm's MDE is 0.025 against an effect of 0.029: adequate, not comfortable. ⚠ **`#372`: and about half of that is simply that those people started earlier at everything.** Adding mean onset age to the same model drops the ordering coefficient from **−0.0287** to **−0.0148**, a **48.4%** drop (bootstrap 95% CI [+31.4%, +83.8%]) against an offset floor of −0.11% ± 5.01% built from a synthetic control with the same correlation to the ordering but none to shame — **+9.69 sd**. **And what remains does not survive on its own**: −0.0148 against a same-model permutation threshold of 0.0173 (−1.65 sd). **So the ordering is not yet an independent route** — half of it is overall earliness and the remainder is below this design's resolution. The description above still holds; the mechanism does not separate. ⚠ Wide and narrow calibers are **different populations** (`#346`), so this is a cross-population replication, not added power. ⚠ The first version of that wide arm **was not wide**: `S` is only defined at coverage ≥8, so requiring it re-imposed the narrow rule and the arm grew by 70 people instead of 5,000 — an inclusion-rule relaxation that does not move n never happened, and is a scope claim in the flattering direction. ⚠ **`#334`: three routes, and only one pair overlaps.** Onset-early carries an increment of **0.484pp** against S's **0.491pp** and c3⁻'s **0.949pp**. Over the three pre-declared pairwise interactions, permuted 1000 times with a family-wise max-|t| threshold of 2.465: `S × c3⁻` **p_fw = 0.769** and `c3⁻ × EARLY` **p_fw = 0.987** are additive, while **`S × EARLY` is sub-additive (|t| = 3.36, p_fw = 0.004)** and clears the threshold under all three numeric codings of the onset bins. Rare taste and early arrival overlap; the other two pairs simply add. ⚠ **`#337`: and the overlap survives at the person×category level** (147,387 cells, 13,517 people, permutation null over people): being **earlier than that category's own population median** (+0.082) and the **category's rarity** (+0.068) each carry shame, and their interaction is **−0.0094 (permutation p = 0.010)** — so the overlap is not the shared developmental schedule, which is centred out cell by cell. ⚠ **`#350`: it replicates in a larger, more heterogeneous sample.** At a looser inclusion rule (≥4 of the 32 blocks rather than ≥8, n = 6,717 → 8,196) the same family-wise test gives **|t| = 4.47, p_fw = 0.000** for `S × EARLY` (from 3.36 / 0.005), with the other two interactions still null (p_fw ≥ 0.658). ⚠ **The two calibers are different populations, not the same one with more data** — low-coverage respondents differ systematically (`#346`), so this is a replication across populations, not an increase in power. ⚠ **`#357`: two of those four moderators were deliberately coarsened before release.** The dataset's own column notes — a file this project had never read until now — record that *"How sexually liberated was your upbringing?"* was collapsed from **7 levels to 3**, and relationship style from **3 to 2**. Coarsening a moderator attenuates interaction detection, so the per-variable MDE of 10–30% is partly the release's doing, not the population's. The publisher also states the anonymization leaves correlations **~25% weaker than the original** — so every correlation on this page is a **lower bound**. ⚠ **`#373`: and that card is not describing this exact file.** Its header states 15,511 × 376; the CSV is **15,503 × 365**. Of the five columns whose level counts the card states outright, **two match and three do not**: `straightness` is documented at 5 levels and released at **2**; `childhood_adversity` and `TotalMentalIllness` are documented as None/Any and released with **only the positive label**. So the coarsening claims above are what the **card** says, not what this file **is** — and since the one checkable divergence went further than documented (5 → 2), the direction is right and the **magnitude is a lower bound**. ✅ One consequence runs the other way and improves an earlier caveat: because the card documents those two flags as None/Any and the row count rules out rows having been dropped, the file’s blanks **are** the None level, so reading them as zero is correct and `#366`’s warning about conflating “none” with “no answer” is withdrawn. ⚠ **`#374`: but the card is stale, not false — its deletions hold.** Its `DROPPED GT QUESTIONS` section names 13 identifiers as removed, and under a sound matcher (whole identifier-segment equality, positive-controlled at 8/8 on columns known to be present) **0 of the 13 survive in the file**. A first pass using bare substring matching claimed 2 survivors — `race` matching `Totalsexyrace`/`raceplay` and `smost` matching a question that merely *references* `bodypartsmost` — the classic unsound direction: absence of a substring implies absence of the column, presence does not imply presence. ⚠ The card uses the original survey's names, so a rename would also read as absent; this check can prove the list wrong, not prove it right. **Read the card as accurate about what was removed and out of date about how much was collapsed.**
> sexes** (`#285` `#286`). Rebuilt independently in each group (n = 3,232 / 3,485): the block-level
> loading subspace replicates at **0.7695** across the sex split against **0.8385 ± 0.0263** across a
> random split of the same sizes (**2.6×**), and — the sharper test — the *joint R² curve over all 29
> outcomes* correlates **+0.6667** across the sex split against **+0.8728 ± 0.0161** across a random one
> (**12.8×**). Both ends of the positive control behave (a sex-driven synthetic quantity gives −0.2553;
> `agreeableness`, which correlates −0.0749 with sex, gives −0.0016). The three outcomes that differ
> most: `I sometimes find peop…` **1.9% vs 5.0%**, `I find the thought of…` **3.2% vs 0.6%**, `animated`
> **6.4% vs 8.9%**. ⚠ This does **not** say any column is wrong — every one was controlled for sex. It
> says **the table is an average, and that average hides a resolvable disagreement.**

> ⚠ **`form_i` is the only standing dimension that does *not* reach shame** (`#278`). Everything else in
> this project ends up pointing at it — position +0.1185 [cov≥8 · scope stated], breadth-type c3 +0.1286, the between-block
> contrast on the disjoint instrument +0.1384 — and this one does not: its correlation with shame is
> +0.0454, but its **increment over the six coordinates is +0.21 ± 0.13pp, indistinguishable from zero.**
> **When every quantity in a project points at the same outcome, the informative one is the quantity that
> doesn't.** ⚠ **`#316`: that is a statement about the *increment*, and the wider reading is wrong.** Un-orthogonalised, `form` correlates with shame at **+0.0797**, and its Spearman–Brown reliability is only **0.380** (`animated`↔`written` = **+0.2345** at n = 6,478; ⚠ **`#375`: this page previously printed +0.468 here, which is `2r`, the numerator of Spearman–Brown, mislabelled as `r` — SB(0.468) would be 0.638, not 0.380, and 2 × 0.2346 = 0.469. The reliability 0.380 was always right; the pair correlation was not.**), so the disattenuated value is **+0.1293** (bootstrap 95% interval **[+0.088, +0.167]**, `#320`) — the same size as position (+0.1185 [cov≥8 · scope stated]) and breadth-type c3 (+0.1286). It survives controlling response style (**+0.0791** disattenuated, 61% retained). What is absent is not the signal but its *unique* part: after removing both the six coordinates and response style, **+0.0168**, below this design's MDE of 0.0348. **`form` is not a shame-free dimension; it is a dimension this survey measures too coarsely.** ⚠ **`#419`: the direction of these two items has now been checked, and so — for the first time — has the direction of the shame item itself.** Both `animated`/`written` and the shame question are `LIKERT_PM3` (symmetric, −2…+2 and −3…+3), structurally unlike the all-negative `RATING_NEG_FIB` scale whose direction `#392` had to reverse. Calibrating the whole family with an item whose direction is forced by its wording — *I have acted upon or experimented with all the things that arouse me*, which must rise with the count of sex acts a person endorses if higher means more agreement — gives **+0.118** and **+0.056** against the two count anchors. **So higher does mean more agreement, and every shame result on this page rests on an assumption that had never been verified until now.** ⚠ Separately, the two count anchors **disagree** about these items — arousal by drawn and written material goes with **fewer** endorsed sex acts (−0.076, −0.062) and **more** fetish categories (+0.095, +0.035) — which is a substantive split, not an instrument failure. ⚠ **`#420`: and it survives a proper test.** With `S`, c3⁻, shame and age controlled, the form score predicts **−0.076** on the count of endorsed sex acts (threshold 0.019) and **+0.049** on the count of fetish categories (threshold 0.022), both past offset nulls built by permuting the form score itself — **opposite signs, both surviving**, against a backdrop where the two counts correlate +0.373 with each other, so collinearity residue was not a straw man. **People who respond to drawn and written material want more kinds of things and have done fewer of them — and this is not overall rarity of taste, which is controlled.** ⚠ Both counts are self-reported and standardised, so only signs and threshold-crossings are compared; the split is a description, not a causal claim. ⚠ **`#421`: and "breadth" on this page is not one quantity.** Enumerating the five breadth measures actually used — onset-category count, fetish-category count, endorsed-sex-act count, block coverage and total picks — only **6 of their 10 pairwise correlations reach 0.6**. Onset-category count, block coverage and total picks intercorrelate at **+0.83 to +0.86** and behave as one thing (how much of the survey a person engaged with); fetish-category count sits at its edge (+0.62 to +0.74); and **the endorsed-sex-act count is the outlier, correlating only +0.26 to +0.51 with the rest** — its weakest link being with fetish-category count at **+0.2615**. **So "breadth" is at least two things — how many kinds of thing interest you, and how much you have actually done — and the form score above pushes those two in opposite directions.** ⚠ **`#422`: and the ambiguity turns out to be bounded, so one definition fixes it rather than ninety annotations.** Auditing both pages, about 90 uses of breadth-like wording do not name a specific measure within their immediate context — but **every one of the 15 places where the odd-one-out measure appears (7 here, 8 in the Chinese page) names it explicitly**, as `Totalsexacts` or "endorsed sex acts". **So a bare "breadth" on this page never means the sex-act count.**

⚠ **`#423`: and enumerating the question types turned up a loaded trap that has not yet fired, plus one fact about people.** The survey has two Fibonacci-coded rating families whose value sets are exact mirrors — `{0,−1,−2,−3,−5,−8}` and `{0,1,2,3,5,8}`. `#392` established that **−8 is the most erotic end** of the first. Anchoring the second against the same two counts whose direction is fixed by being counts, it comes out **positive on both** (+0.166 and +0.114, against a permute-within-mask null at 7.4 and 4.9 sd) while the first reproduces its **−0.450 / −0.186** in the same script. **So magnitude is arousal in both families and the sign is decorative — using them by raw numeric value would give opposite signs for the same thing.** The second family is used nowhere in this project (0 of its 7 items), which is exactly why nobody caught it: **an unused variable's direction is not a safe assumption, it is an unchecked one.** ⚠ And now that the direction is anchored, those seven items say something: they ask about finding **your own body** erotic, and the people who do endorse **more** sex acts and **more** fetish categories — **an erotic relation to one's own body goes with a wider sexual life, not a narrower one.** ⚠ Scope: only **2,061** people answered that block (13% of the sample, and the item wording selects on body type), so this is a statement about them, not about everyone here.

⚠ **`#424`: and there is no specialist taste in this survey.** The largest never-anchored question family — 67 arousal ratings, everything from anal to spanking to mind-break — was anchored against the same two counts. **122 of 134 tests clear a family-wise threshold, every one of them positive, and not one of the 134 is negative at all**, from +0.028 at the weakest to +0.372 at the strongest. ⚠ The obvious objection is that these 67 items intercorrelate, so a single shared factor landing positively by chance would turn them all positive at once. **That objection is real and it was measured**: under a null that moves whole person-rows together — preserving every column-to-column correlation exactly (0.0757 against 0.0757) while destroying the relation to the counts — the number of negatives ranges from **34 to 104 with a median of 67**, a spread about twice what independent signs would give, **and zero negatives never occurred in 200 draws.** **So the reading survives its own strongest objection: no taste here, at any intensity, goes with a narrower sexual life. More intense is never more specialised.** ⚠ "Wider" is defined by two count measures, this is cross-sectional, and the weakest item is close enough to zero that "never negative" is the claim — not "everything goes together".

⚠ **`#429`: and one signed claim on this page rests on a direction that cannot be checked here at all.** Enumerating the last unexamined question family turned up eight ordered variables this project uses whose numeric direction was never established — the five Big Five traits, powerlessness, porn habit and a bondage average. That matters because three passages above read them **as words**: "less extraverted, more neurotic, less agency". **Extraversion is now established**: it correlates **+0.146** with the banded partner count, and the literature's sign for that pair is positive, so higher means more extraverted — **and that holds under four different codings of the band variable** (ordinal ranks, band midpoints, log midpoints, and a binary none-versus-any that discards the bands entirely): **+0.115 to +0.146, same sign every time.** **The other four Big Five traits inherit it at one remove** — all five share an identical n, range and step (15,503 · −6 to +6 · 1), so they are one scoring pipeline, but that is an inference about the pipeline, **not a measurement of each trait**. ⚠ **`#481` tests whether it could have been a measurement, and it could not — for a reason worth naming.** Every direction-established quantity on this page was put into the anchor set and the four traits rerun against the ones **outside their own scoring pipeline**: their strongest reach is **0.075**, **0.069**, **0.052** and **0.031**, all under the bar that porn use (0.196) and the bondage average (0.247) clear. **Extraversion itself is excluded on purpose**: it shares the pipeline, so a common coding convention would reproduce the expected sign whatever the truth was. **And that is the circularity: the very thing that licenses the inference — one shared scoring pipeline — is the thing that makes it untestable from inside.** The inheritance is therefore not a shortcut taken in preference to a measurement; it is the only route this release leaves open. ⚠ Sign priors for all four were written down before any correlation was computed, and three of the four were recorded as **having none** — so even a cleared bar would have bought power without a direction. **Powerlessness gets neither**: its range is −9 to +9, so it is not that pipeline, and this release contains no direction-fixed quantity to anchor it against — the one candidate, a mental-illness total, turns out to be a string with the single value "Any". **So "less agency" is UNVERIFIED here: the magnitude stands, the word does not.** ⚠ **`#461` retried it under the corrected rule and the verdict holds, now with a number.** The rule that produced the original verdict was later found broken (`#442`), so the retry used every quantity on this page whose direction is fixed by construction — endorsed sex acts, fetish categories, and the banded partner count. Three variables whose direction **is** established reach **0.196**, **0.247** and **0.146** against that set; **powerlessness reaches 0.074 at its best**. **So it is not that the test was too strict — it is that nothing available fixes which end of this variable is which.** ⚠ **`#480` finds an anchor that the count-only rule had never tried, and it changes the answer — at two removes.** Asking a cheaper question first, whether the variable is simply a noisy copy of one of the three quantities whose direction is settled: its strongest link to any of them is **−0.174**, while a second measure of one construct would need to be far above **0.5**, and even two different counts in this dataset reach **+0.373**. **So it is not a shadow of the known quantities.** But the variable it is closest to is **extraversion**, whose direction *is* established here — so extraversion itself is a legitimate anchor, and **−0.174 clears the same bar the counts failed**. Read against the standard position that agency is a facet of extraversion, the coding resolves: **higher on this variable means less agency**, and the page's earlier wording was right after all. ⚠ Two cautions carried with it, both material: extraversion's own direction rests on a literature prior, so this is a **second** prior chained onto a first; and **the prior for this pair was written down after the correlation was seen**, not before, which is exactly the order this project treats as a warning elsewhere. The direction is therefore reported as readable, not as pre-registered. ⚠ And even a strong anchor would only settle direction if a prior fixed the expected sign of the relation, which for "powerlessness against how many sex acts someone endorses" there is not; that was written down before the numbers were read. ⚠ A literature prior is a weaker anchor than the wording-locked item used for the shame scale, and it is labelled as one. ⚠ Claims stated as \|r\| — such as "all Big Five \|r\| ≤ 0.056" — are sign-immune and are untouched by any of this.

⚠ **`#433`: and once its direction was settled, the porn-use variable turned out to add exactly one thing.** Against the four outcomes this page already carries — shame, "could I stop", therapeutic, how much acted on — with **rarity of taste, how well ordinary content works, breadth type, category count and age all controlled**, more porn use still predicts **more shame** (**+0.061**, **5.19 sd** against a family-wise threshold of 2.44) and predicts **none of the other three** (1.48, 1.39, 1.02). Those three are **nulls with power**: the same design detects an effect of **0.029**, less than half the shame coefficient. **So what porn use contributes, over and above how unusual or how wide someone's tastes are, is shame — and only shame. Not a sense that it is out of control, not a sense that it heals, not more of it acted on.** ⚠ n = **6,715**, cross-sectional, and the raw associations are small throughout (0.030 to 0.077) — this is a statement about which one survives controls, not about a large effect.

⚠ **`#434`: and shame turns out to be the brake, not the carrier — which is the opposite of what the previous paragraph led me to write.** Having found that porn use predicts shame and nothing else, I wrote that the chain must run use → shame → behaviour. Tested, it runs both ways at once. Porn use reaches "how much I have acted on it" by **two opposing routes**: directly, more use goes with **more** acted (**+0.0171**); through shame, more use goes with more shame and more shame with **less** acted (**+0.0611 × −0.0797 = −0.0049**, past its permutation threshold of 0.0018, bootstrap **[−0.0067, −0.0032]** excluding zero). The net (+0.0122) is smaller than the direct because the two oppose each other. ⚠ **`#435` retracts the share I first reported here.** I wrote that the indirect route cancels **39.8%** of the direct one; bootstrapping that ratio gives **[−3.6, +2.2]**, because its denominator — the total effect, +0.0122 — sits almost on zero, and a ratio with a near-zero denominator is not an estimate. **The indirect effect itself stands** (it clears its own permutation threshold and its own interval excludes zero); **only the fraction does not.**

⚠ **`#435`: and the brake is not a flat tax — it discriminates, and one quantity is braked in the opposite direction.** Running the same three-path split for five quantities at once, with the algebraic identity holding to 2e-16 in every case: **rarity of taste loses 8.1%** of its route to behaviour through shame **[−12.4%, −4.8%]**, while **"ordinary content also works" gains 7.2%** **[+4.2%, +11.9%]** — two intervals that do not overlap. ⚠ The **signs** here are algebra, not evidence: the shame→behaviour segment is shared, so anything that raises shame must lose and anything that lowers it must gain; the panel deliberately includes a quantity with a negative path to shame so that this is visible rather than hidden. **What is not algebra is the size** — and the sizes differ by more than their own resampling spread. ⚠ Two of the five (porn use, category count) have totals too near zero for their share to be estimable at all, and they are reported as such rather than dropped.

⚠ **`#436`: and the one coordinate that points away from doing turns out to point at something else instead.** `c3⁻` — the breadth-type coordinate — is the only one here whose path to "how much I have acted on it" is negative. Split three ways with and without shame in the model: **with shame controlled, what survives is not doing less — it is feeling less able to change what arouses you** (**−0.0347**, z **−2.95** against a family-wise **2.37**, essentially unmoved from −0.0342 without shame). The path to acting less **does not separate from shame at this resolution**: it falls from z −2.82 to **−2.25**, just under the threshold, with the coefficient itself dropping only a fifth. **That is a threshold crossing, not a collapse.** ⚠ **`#437` resolves it by asking whether the threshold itself is the limit.** At ten times the permutations the threshold settles at **2.384** with its own bootstrap interval **[2.323, 2.434]** and a drift of **1.7%** between 2,000 and 4,000 draws, while the observed **2.276** sits below that whole interval. **So the answer is not that acting-less is absent — it is that this design cannot separate it from shame**, and that is now a measured statement rather than an open one. ⚠ It also means the family-wise construction used throughout this page is stable at this scale, which was the outcome that would have forced every number built on it to be restated.

⚠ **`#439`: and feeling unable to change what arouses you has two roads, not one.** This page already says that what shapes "I can't stop" is experience rather than emotion — how much someone has acted on it predicts the belief (**−0.039**) while shame does not. Breadth type predicts it too (**−0.035**), and at almost the same size, so the obvious reading is that it works through experience. It does not. Decomposed with acting as the mediator, the route through experience carries **−2.4%** of the total **[−6.4%, −0.5%]** — and the minus sign is the point: it runs the **opposite** way. Breadth type goes with having done **less** (−0.027), and doing less goes with feeling **more** able to change, so that route should be pulling the other way. It is, and it cancels almost none of the direct path (**−0.036**, larger than the total). **So people high on breadth type feel they cannot change this even though the one thing about them that ought to make it feel changeable — that they have acted on it less — is also true of them.** ⚠ The pre-registered kill here was mis-worded: it asked whether the indirect effect differs from zero, which it does, rather than whether it carries the total, which it does not; both readings are kept in the record rather than the first being quietly replaced. ⚠ n = 6,715, cross-sectional, shame in the model throughout; the family threshold moved 3.3% between 400 and 4,000 permutations.

⚠ **`#440`: and it is not what you like — it is how your liking is distributed.** The direct path from breadth type to "I cannot change this" was the largest unexplained thing on this page, so it was taken apart at the level of the object: the coordinate was rebuilt 32 times, once with each block of the survey removed, sign-aligned to a common reference, and its coefficient recomputed each time. **No block carries it.** The median block moves the coefficient by **0.25** of its own bootstrap spread and the largest by **1.86** — inside the **2.24** that the maximum of 32 reaches by resampling alone. Dropping the top one through six blocks by loading leaves the coefficient between **−0.038 and −0.045**, never weaker than the **−0.036** it starts at. **So the feeling that this cannot be changed does not come from any particular content. It is carried by the shape of a person's pattern across the whole survey, not by what is in it.** ⚠ The pre-registered kill here would have published the opposite: it compared a maximum over 32 blocks against a one-standard-deviation bar, which a maximum clears almost surely, and it named block 11 as the answer. The corrected bar is the null distribution of the maximum. Both are on the record. ⚠ The drop-k curve is an approximation rather than a true leave-k-out refit, and is reported as corroboration, not as the test.

⚠ **`#441` narrows this: the shape is not the breadth-type contrast, and that contrast is the weakest of the three.** `c3⁻` is the third eigenvector of the block-by-block residual correlation; the obvious next question was what the first two are and whether they predict the belief as well. **All three do.** Against a bar built from the largest of the three under permutation (**0.028**), the first component reaches **0.052**, the second **0.053**, and the breadth-type third only **0.036**. **So feeling unable to change what arouses you goes with the leading residual structure being strong in general, not with one particular contrast** — and the sentence above is right that it is not about content, but wrong to leave the impression that breadth type is the carrier. ⚠ **`#442` corrects this**: the first component was called unanchorable because its correlation with **one** direction-fixed count is **+0.028** — but against the **other** count, fetish categories, it reaches **−0.131**. The one-anchor rule was the error, and its own positive control says so: on this mask a variable whose direction is already established, porn use, reaches only **+0.099** against the first count and **+0.139** against the second. **A rule that declares a known-direction variable unanchorable is measuring the rule, not the variable.** With the corrected rule — clearing the bar on either count — all three components are anchored, and the first one reads: **more fetish categories, less sense that this can be changed.** ⚠ The second correlates **−0.38** with the count of endorsed sex acts, so a large part of what it is may be overall involvement rather than a distinct contrast; that is a caveat, not a result. ⚠ Each component was anchored separately — an eigenvector's sign is arbitrary, and this project has published a backwards reading off one four times.

⚠ **`#442`: and it is not involvement wearing a different name — controlling involvement makes it stronger.** The obvious deflation of the paragraph above is that the second component correlates **−0.38** with the count of endorsed sex acts, so "the residual structure predicts feeling stuck" might just be "being more involved predicts it". Adding that count to the controls and refitting, with both arms reported: the three coefficients go from **0.052 / 0.053 / 0.035** to **0.052 / 0.062 / 0.047** — none collapses and two **grow**, the breadth-type one by **31%**. **So involvement was masking the relationship rather than producing it, and what the leading residual structure tracks is something involvement does not contain.** ⚠ I predicted the second component would collapse. ⚠ **`#443` gives it a first shape: there are two ways of being more involved, and they land on different people-facts.** The second and third components are both anchored by the same count — endorsed sex acts — but with opposite signs, so the structure cuts involvement in half. Turned to face the same way and run against the four outcomes, each half clears the shared bar (**0.034**) in **exactly one cell, and they are different cells**. The first half reaches **feeling less able to change what arouses you** (**−0.054**) with essentially nothing on shame (+0.010). The second half reaches **less shame** (**−0.088**, the largest coefficient in the table) with the agency cell pointing the **other way** (+0.034 — a whisker under the bar, so recorded as unresolved rather than as absence). The two profiles correlate **−0.21**, bootstrap **[−0.54, +0.16]**, which rules out their being the same thing read twice. **So being more involved is not one condition: one form of it costs you the sense that this is changeable and no shame, the other costs shame and, if anything, returns some of that sense.** ⚠ Four points make a profile, so the interval is the result and the point estimate is not. ⚠ Both components' signs are stated in their anchored frame, never in the one the eigensolver happened to return.

⚠ **`#444`: the half that costs you the sense of changeability is loaded on the bodily-fluid questions — and the test that first said so could not have failed.** Asking what the two halves are made of, the obvious comparison is the correlation between their two loading vectors, which came out **−0.01**. That number is worthless: they are eigenvectors of the same symmetric matrix, so they are orthogonal by construction and the comparison could only ever have returned zero. Replaced with a test that can fail — how much their top-loading block sets overlap, against what two random orthogonal vectors would give — the answer is **inside the null at every cut** (overlaps of 1, 1, 2, 4, 4 for the top 4 to 12), so **"they are made of different blocks" is unresolved, not established.** What does survive is narrower and about content: taking the six blocks whose own wording names a bodily fluid, their loading on the first half averages **+0.254** against **−0.092** for the other twenty-six — a gap of **+0.346** at **4.3 sd** of a block-label permutation — while on the second half the same six sit **inside** the null (−1.7 sd). ⚠ This is a description, not a name: an ordering of loadings has twice been turned into a construct on this page and killed both times. ⚠ The six-block set was noticed in this data before it was formalised. **`#445` puts that to the test and it holds**: re-estimating the loadings on twenty independent random halves of the respondents and evaluating the gap on each half's own permutation null, it clears in **20 of 20**, every gap positive, median **+0.313** against a half-sample minimum detectable effect of **0.158** — so the effect is **2.2×** what a half can resolve, and a failure to replicate would have been visible. ⚠ This is split-half replication, not held-out data: the same respondents were in the sample where the pattern was first seen, so the concern is reduced rather than removed. ⚠ **`#446` measures what that twenty was actually resting on, and it is the content rather than the coordinate.** The twenty half-sample loading vectors agree with each other at a median of **+0.86**, but their spread runs from **−0.18** to **+0.97** — the coordinate is reproducible in most halves and collapses in a few. The fluid gap tracks that (**+0.58**), yet in the five halves where the coordinate is worst recovered it is **still positive in all five**, median **+0.19** — above the half-sample detectable effect of **0.158**, though only just. **So the content pattern is the sturdier of the two: the coordinate is a carrier that sometimes fails to re-form, and the fluid blocks stay together anyway.** ⚠ At the edge of resolution, so this ranks the two rather than separating them. ⚠ **`#447` says which part of the coordinate is the fragile one, and it is not the part the obvious explanation predicted.** The natural account of a coordinate failing to re-form is that the second and third eigenvalues sit close and the two components trade places — a relabelling, not a change of structure. That is refuted by its own sign: the eigenvalue gap and the per-vector agreement correlate **−0.37**, so the halves that recover the vector worst are the ones with the *larger* gap, which is the opposite of what a swap predicts. Compared as two-dimensional subspaces instead — using principal angles, which no relabelling can affect — **the smaller of the two angles never exceeds 22° in any of the twenty halves** (median 12°), while the larger one stays under 45° in **18 of 20** and reaches the random-subspace level (**82°**, against a null whose 5th percentile is **77°**) in one. **So one direction of the pair re-forms every single time and the second one fails outright in about one half in twenty** — the pair is not equally solid in both of its directions, and claims resting on it inherit that asymmetry. ⚠ **`#448`: and the fluid pattern sits on the *less* preserved of the two, which makes it more independent of the coordinate rather than less.** Pairing each half's plane against the full-sample plane and reading off which direction each half preserves best, the fluid signature on the better-preserved direction reaches a median of **1.8** standard deviations of its block-label null, while on the worse-preserved one it reaches **4.3** **[2.5, 4.8]**. **So the content that replicated in all twenty halves is carried by the part of the coordinate that reconstructs least reliably** — the two are not the same fact, and the content does not depend on the coordinate being well conditioned. ⚠ I predicted the opposite. ⚠ One limit worth stating: all twenty recovered directions lie inside the same two-plane by construction, so their agreement with one another is not evidence of independent recovery and is not used as any. ⚠ **`#449`: and asking the content directly — no eigenvectors at all — costs most of the sample and does not settle it.** A quantity built purely from wording, how much a person picks in the six fluid blocks relative to the other twenty-six, correlates **+0.48** with the coordinate, so it is the same construct family. But requiring those blocks to have been answered drops n from **6,715 to 2,490**, and at that size the design loses its specificity: a **sham** six-block split, chosen at random, clears the same bar in **7 of 20 tries**. The two outcomes that come closest (shame **+0.048**, the changeability item **−0.050**) sit exactly on a threshold that arbitrary splits clear a third of the time, so **this is recorded as unresolved, not as a negative result.** ⚠ The useful part is the sample arithmetic: the coordinate is not an ornament over a simpler question — it is what lets the analysis keep the two-thirds of respondents who never answered those blocks. ⚠ **`#450` repairs that arm's bar and the answer flips depending on which null is asked — which is the point.** The same observation, **0.0497**, clears the bar built by shuffling people (**0.0468**) and does **not** clear the bar built by drawing random six-block splits (**0.0725**). **The two nulls exclude different worlds:** shuffling people asks whether this quantity relates to the outcome at all; redrawing the split asks whether *these six* blocks are special rather than any six. For a claim about particular content, only the second question is the one being made — and under it, at n = 2,490, **any six blocks would do.** ⚠ This does not touch the loading result above, which was tested against a block-label null from the start and reached **4.3 sd** with **20 of 20** replications at full sample. ⚠ **`#451` joins the two strongest arcs on this page for the first time: what arrived early feels more fixed.** One arc says things that arrived earlier go with more shame; the other, built over the last several rounds, says a person can feel unable to change what arouses them for reasons that are not shame, not experience, not involvement, and not any single kind of content. Putting mean onset age against the four outcomes with current age held — the two correlate **+0.32**, so that control is not optional — **later onset goes with feeling more able to change** (**+0.050** against a family-wise **0.031**, and a detectable effect of **0.015**), while the shame cell reproduces what the page already knew (**−0.060**: later onset, less shame) and serves as the positive control that the instrument is measuring the right thing. **So the sense that this cannot be changed is partly a fact about when it arrived** — the earlier something showed up, the less a person feels able to move it. ⚠ Onset ages are recalled, not recorded. **`#452` checks whether that can produce the result, and it cannot — by the construction of the project's own recall-bias measurement.** The bias this dataset actually measures (`#114`: people report their more highly rated categories as beginning earlier) is defined *after* subtracting each person's overall precocity — and that precocity correlates **+0.993** with the mean onset age used here. **So the measured bias lives in a layer that is almost exactly what this focal has already had removed from it.** As a further check, adding overall rating intensity as a stand-in covariate — labelled a stand-in, not the real control — leaves the coefficient at **+0.048** from **+0.050**, retaining **95%**. ⚠ The separation is not total: the residual within-person tilt still correlates **+0.27** with mean onset. ⚠ Cross-sectional. ⚠ **`#454`: and it is a gradient, not a cliff.** Cut into the four onset strata this page already uses — median onset **12.5 / 14.7 / 16.7 / 19.9** years, reproduced exactly and in equal quarters — the adjusted sense of being able to change what arouses you runs **−0.060 → −0.016 → +0.007 → +0.068**, strictly increasing, with the first and last intervals not overlapping. Rank agreement with the ordering is **+1.00** against a label-shuffle null whose 95th percentile is **+0.80**. The threshold alternative does not fit: the earliest stratum sits **−0.080** from the rest, but the other three spread **0.084** among themselves, so nothing singles out the early end. **So this is not "before some age it is fixed" — every step later in onset comes with a little more sense that it could move.** ⚠ With four strata, perfect ordering is the strongest result available and its own floor is p ≈ 0.042, so the ordering test is at its resolution limit; the gradient itself is the substance. ⚠ The cuts are the page's own, not chosen here. ⚠ **`#455`: and shame runs the same gradient in mirror image** — **`#456` withdraws the reading that this means one antecedent.** Across the identical four strata, with the identical controls, adjusted shame runs **+0.073 → +0.049 → −0.047 → −0.074** — perfectly ordered, opposite in sign, exactly as the page's older result implies. Both directions were fixed by prior findings before this round, so each is tested one-sided: **p = 0.040** for changeability and **0.042** for shame. Their steepnesses are **+0.128** and **−0.147** on the same scale and the difference, **0.019**, cannot be resolved — **but the interval on that difference, [−0.109, +0.073], is wider than either steepness itself, so this is a resolution limit and not a finding of sameness.** **What can be said: one thing about when a person's tastes arrived moves both how ashamed they are and how movable it feels, in opposite directions and at sizes this design cannot tell apart.** ⚠ A two-sided ordering test cannot fire at four strata at all — a perfect order arises in 2 of 24 random arrangements, so the 95th percentile of \|rho\| is 1. That is why the direction had to be pre-specified, and it qualifies the paragraph above as well.

⚠ **`#456`: the two consequences have nothing to do with each other, and saying "one antecedent" was reading more into that than it holds.** Holding shame while re-running the changeability gradient moves it from **+0.128** to **+0.128** — retention **1.007** — and holding changeability while re-running shame moves it from **−0.147** to **−0.147**, retention **1.005**. Both stay perfectly ordered. **But that near-zero mediation is close to forced**: the two outcomes correlate **−0.002** with each other, so controlling either was never going to remove anything from the other, and the test is much weaker than it looks. **What the arithmetic does support is narrower and worth having: when a person's tastes arrived is tied to two things — how ashamed they are, and how movable it feels — and those two things are unrelated to one another.** That matches what this page already found from the other direction: shame changes what people do, not what they believe they could stop. ⚠ So the timing is one fact with two unrelated consequences, not one mechanism with two faces, and this round cannot tell which of those the world is. ⚠ **`#457` turns that caveat into a number that can be computed before the test is run.** How far controlling one outcome can possibly move the other's coefficient is fixed by three correlations, so it is knowable in advance: for these two arms the bound is **0.74%** and **0.36%** of the total effect, and what the full fits actually moved was **0.7%** and **0.5%**. The same calculation says the page's porn-use mediation **does** have room, so this is not a rule that refuses everything. **A mediation test whose bound is a fraction of a percent cannot fail, and that is now checked before the round rather than noticed after it.**

⚠ **`#458`: and the two paths do come apart — three of six page quantities touch exactly one of them.** Running all six person-level quantities this page already carries against both outcomes at once, with a family-wise bar of **0.034**: rarity of taste (**+0.079 / −0.086**), "ordinary content also works" (**−0.066 / −0.127**) and onset age (**−0.058 / +0.050**) move **both**; **breadth type** (**+0.082 / −0.030**) and **porn use** (**+0.059 / −0.016**) move **only shame**; and **how many categories a person answered** (**+0.010 / −0.035**) moves **only the sense of changeability**. **So being ashamed of what arouses you and feeling unable to move it are not two readings of one state: what you are drawn to and how much you use pornography reach the shame and stop there.** ⚠ The category count looked like the weakest link — a measure of how much of the survey someone walked through. **`#459` tests that and the volume reading does not hold.** Of the five engagement measures this page enumerates, only two move the changeability outcome: the onset-category count (**−0.035**) and **block coverage** (**−0.042**), both counts of **how many distinct areas a person entered**. The three that count *within* areas — total picks (**−0.023**), fetish categories (**−0.014**) and endorsed sex acts (**−0.031**) — clear nothing, against a shared bar of **0.034**. **So it is not that people who answered more feel more able to change: sheer number of things ticked says nothing. What tracks it is the number of separate domains a person has been into at all.** ⚠ **`#460` then splits answering behaviour against itself and the artifact reading does not survive.** Two quantities are built from the same two numbers: **width**, how many separate domains a person entered, and **depth**, how many options they ticked per domain entered. Both are produced by answering, so a general tendency to answer a lot would show in both. Run together — they correlate **+0.41**, so neither is eating the other — **width predicts the changeability outcome (−0.036) and depth predicts nothing at all** (**+0.004** and **−0.023** against a bar of **0.030**). **So what tracks feeling able to change is not how much someone answered but how many separate territories they have been into; how thoroughly they filled in each one says nothing.**

⚠ **`#463`: and the two leading components of the residual structure fall on the two different paths — the first time both sides of the fork can be named.** Adding the first component, oriented so that higher means **more fetish categories** (the frame the paragraph above uses), to the same seven-quantity model: it reaches the changeability outcome (**−0.047**) and not shame (**+0.017**), while **breadth type still reaches shame (+0.078) and not changeability**, against a shared bar of **0.034**. **So where a person spends their openness goes with how ashamed they are; how many separate fetish categories they hold goes with how movable it feels.
