Two different questions about the same tastes, landing in different places.** ⚠ In this model the category count falls just under the bar (**−0.031**) where in the model without the first component it cleared — the two models are not comparable, and the earlier claim is scoped to its own rather than withdrawn. ⚠ **`#464` corrects the name attached to the first component, though not its direction.** Checking every countable quantity on this page against all three components: the first component's four anchors all point the same way, so its orientation stands — but its strongest are **total picks (0.227)**, **block coverage (0.213)** and **onset categories (0.183)**, with fetish categories the **weakest of the four at 0.131**. **So it is not "fetish breadth" — it is the same overall-engagement-breadth direction that block coverage and the category count mark, and it lands on the changeability path exactly where they do.** The sentence above should be read as engagement breadth, not fetish content. ⚠ The same check kills a guess of mine in the other direction: the breadth-type component was supposed to be the one with no countable correspondent, and it has the **strongest** of the three (**0.460** with the endorsed-sex-act count). **So the five failed attempts to name it did not fail for want of an external correlate**, and whatever the reason is, it is not that. ⚠ **`#465` narrows what a name for it would have to survive.** Taking that strongest correlate — the endorsed-sex-act count — out of the breadth-type coordinate altogether: it accounts for **21%** of the coordinate's variance, and the residual still predicts shame at **+0.069** where the original was **+0.078**, keeping **87%**, and still predicts the changeability outcome not at all. **So what a person's openness is spent on is related to how much they have actually done, but is not that: strip out how many sex acts they endorse and seven-eighths of the link to shame is still there.** ⚠ Which means the unnamed thing is not that count in disguise, and any name offered for it has to survive the same subtraction. ⚠ **`#466` removes every countable quantity at once, and two things come out.** The six counts on this page **jointly** account for only **31%** of the breadth-type coordinate — the strongest alone was 21% — so **69% of it is untouched by any of them**, and after removing all six the link to shame keeps **74%** of its size (**+0.059** from **+0.079**). **So there is a substantial piece of "where a person spends their openness" that no count in this survey reaches, and three-quarters of the shame relation lives there.** ⚠ The second thing qualifies the paragraph above rather than the first: with the counts stripped out, the residual **also** clears on the changeability outcome (**−0.031**), where the coordinate as built did not. So the clean one-path-each separation is a property of the coordinate **as constructed**, not of its count-free core — the counts were carrying the changeability side. ⚠ **`#469`: and the rarity–shame link this page rests on turns out to be twice as strong in domains fewer people enter.** A separate line of work on this repository, run against 186 societies rather than people, argues that condemnation tracks rarity only where norms can actually suppress a behaviour — and that sexual interest is not such a domain. That is marked there as a reading generated by the data, not an established one; this is the person-level corollary tested here. Splitting the survey's blocks by how often their options are picked at all, and computing rarity **within** each block so the split is not the thing being measured: the slope from rarity to shame is **+0.042** in the sixteen most-entered domains and **+0.096** in the sixteen least-entered, a difference of **+0.053** with a bootstrap interval of **[+0.019, +0.090]**. **So being drawn to rare things costs more shame when few people go there at all — the coupling is not uniform across the map.** ⚠ The difference grows monotonically with where the split is placed (**−0.001** to **+0.074** across five cut points, four of them positive), so the size is cut-dependent and the whole curve is reported rather than one cell. ⚠ Rarer blocks give each person fewer picks to average over, which makes their rarity score noisier and would **attenuate** their slope — so the direction found here is the conservative one. ⚠ This is person-level evidence about this survey's population; it can agree or disagree with a society-level claim but cannot stand in for one. ⚠ **`#470` pins down what is robust here and what is not.** The obvious objection to the paragraph above is that as the split moved, the uncommon group also grew from eleven blocks to twenty-one, so the trend could be group size rather than commonness. Rebuilt with the two groups always holding the **same number of blocks** — the k most-entered against the k least-entered, middle discarded — and swept over k = 6, 8, 10, 12, 14: **the difference is positive at every k**, but it is **not monotone** and it ranges from **+0.020 to +0.077**, with three of the five intervals excluding zero. **So the direction survives every specification tried — nine of ten across both sweeps — while the size is not estimable to better than about a factor of four.** The dip at k = 12 (**+0.020**) is noise, and **`#471` establishes that rather than leaving it open**: removing each of the twenty-four blocks in turn at k = 12 moves the difference by at most **0.031**, against a **0.074** bar built from the largest such move under resampling — no single block explains the dip, and the neighbouring k = 11 and k = 13 both sit back up at **+0.044** and **+0.054**. ⚠ The attenuation argument is now measured rather than asserted: at equal block counts the uncommon group still gives each person **5.7–6.5** picks per block against **6.8–8.0** for the common one, so its score is the noisier of the two and the noise runs against the finding. ⚠ **`#472`: and this happens to shame and to nothing else.** Running the identical design — same blocks, same split, same equal-count sweep, same people — against the other outcome instead: the difference between common and uncommon domains is **+0.060** (median across k) for shame, **three of four intervals excluding zero**, and **+0.005** for the sense of being able to change, **none of four**. That is a null with power: at k = 14 the changeability arm resolves to **±0.034**, comfortably below the **+0.061** shame shows at the same k. **So the fact that rare tastes cost more shame in sparsely-entered corners of the map is about shame specifically — the same corners do nothing to whether a person feels the thing could move.** ⚠ Both arms' coefficients are otherwise as the rest of this page has them; only the between-domain difference dissociates. ⚠ **`#473`: and the same question cannot be put to the other path, which is a limit of the design rather than a result.** Asked of the engagement-breadth coordinate instead — build it from the sparsely-entered blocks and from the densely-entered ones and compare — the difference comes out at **−0.059** and clears at every k. **But the reference arm shows the identical difference on shame (−0.068), within 0.006–0.015 of it at every k**, so what is being detected is not a path being moderated but the two coordinates being different quantities: built from half the blocks each, they correlate only **+0.69** and **+0.81** with the full-sample score. **Splitting the blocks leaves rarity meaning what it meant — rarity is defined inside a block — and does not leave a coordinate meaning what it meant.** The result above stands; the analogous question about the other path is not askable this way, and no answer to it is claimed here. ⚠ **`#474`: and inside the handful of domains almost everyone enters, the relation this page is built on is gone.** Estimating the rarity-to-shame slope in the common group alone, against a null where zero is the right expectation: it runs **−0.055 · −0.039 · +0.040 · +0.076** as "common" is widened from the 6 to the 12 most-entered blocks, **crossing zero between the eighth and the tenth**. The uncommon group over the same sweep is **+0.046 · +0.057 · +0.094 · +0.101**, positive and clearing at every one. **So the link between being drawn to rarer things and feeling ashamed of it is not a property of the map as a whole: it is absent in the few domains nearly everyone visits, and it appears as soon as the set is widened.** ⚠ The negative end clears its null at only one of the four widths (n = 1,435 there), so a reversal is **not** claimed — what is established is that the positive relation is absent at that end and present everywhere else. ⚠ The control behaves: the same instrument on the same people finds the known positive effect in the uncommon group at all four widths. ⚠ **`#475`: and a note about this page's error profile, which is measurable rather than modest.** Seven pre-registered thresholds were mis-specified in this run of work. Every one was caught **in the same round that wrote it**, without exception — because in every case the mistake produced a visibly impossible result: four of four passing, a verdict contradicting its own controls, a number that could not be right. **A threshold that is wrong but returns a plausible verdict would not have been caught.** That asymmetry runs the same way as the danger: a threshold set too strictly shows up as a null, and nulls here are audited for power and minimum detectable effect; one set too loosely shows up as a pass, and passes get written down. **So an undetected error on this page is likelier to be a claim that should not have cleared than a claim wrongly withheld.** Of the seven, five of the six kill-thresholds erred toward passing. From this round on, a threshold that PASSES is asked what result would have made it fail — the mirror of asking a null for its resolution, which this project had only ever asked in one direction. ⚠ **`#476` is the first claim put through it, and it now carries its floor.** The positive slope in the common domains was planted against: an effect of known size was subtracted from the outcome in a monotone ladder (**0, −0.02, −0.05, −0.10**), and the recovered slope tracked the plant **exactly, step for step** — **+0.040 → +0.020 → −0.010 → −0.060** at ten blocks and **+0.076 → +0.056 → +0.026 → −0.024** at twelve. **So the claim would have failed had the true effect been smaller by 0.02 at ten blocks or by 0.05 at twelve** — that is the floor it stands on, and it is now stated rather than assumed. ⚠ The other direction is weaker and is worth saying: to report an actual reversal at twelve blocks, the true relation would have to be pushed past **−0.10**, further than any plant tried here. **Reading a negative sign into that end of the map is therefore something this design could not have supported even if it were true**, which is why the earlier paragraph declines to claim one. ⚠ **`#479`: and that end of the map is the same for everyone there — it is not an average over two kinds of people.** The obvious rescue for a near-zero is that two groups pay opposite costs and cancel. Splitting the people in the most-entered domains by the breadth-type coordinate — a cut this page already carries, with its direction already fixed — the two halves come out **−0.050 and −0.056** at six blocks, **−0.042 and −0.034** at eight, **+0.037 and +0.045** at ten: **same sign every time, and never more than 0.008 apart**. For cancellation to be producing the pooled value, the halves would have to differ by at least **twice** it — between **0.077** and **0.110** here — so the margin against that reading is **9× to 17×**. **So the weak, and at the narrowest cuts reversed, shame cost in the crowded domains is something everyone there shares.** ⚠ Halving the sample raises the resolution needed from **0.030** to **0.042**, and only one of the six half-cells clears its own null — the claim rests on the two halves agreeing, not on each of them being separately established. ⚠ This model holds all six quantities at once, which is not the model earlier paragraphs used; coefficients are not comparable across them. ⚠ A pre-registered arithmetic claim of mine failed its own control here — the products of the two correlation sets sum to **−0.025** where the identity I wrote implied nearer zero, because that identity needs the predictors to be orthogonal and these are not. The result above does not rest on it. ⚠ Cross-sectional. What it settles is narrower and worth stating plainly: **whether a number is significant here depends on which null it is asked against, and the right null is fixed by the world you mean to exclude, not by which one is stricter.** ⚠ Supporting this, the one block in the set that names fluids only in general rather than a specific one is also the one block that does not fit (**−0.047**) — what a wording-driven rule looks like, and not what a rule drawn around high loadings looks like. ⚠ The direction of the "could I change it" item is locked by its own answer wording ("with little effort, yes" is its top code), so this is not resting on an unchecked sign. ⚠ And I predicted the opposite cell would be the one to survive. **So shame does not carry porn use through to behaviour — it subtracts from it.** ⚠ The decomposition is an OLS identity (c = c′ + ab held to 2e-16) and the data are cross-sectional, so these are two routes through a correlation structure, not two causal mechanisms; the same numbers are equally consistent with shame driving use. ⚠ The sentence this replaces was written one round earlier, by me, and was **pre-registered as the outcome I expected not to find** — I predicted no mediation at all. Reading rule, established rather than assumed: **unqualified "breadth" refers to the engagement family — onset-category count, block coverage, total picks and fetish-category count, which intercorrelate +0.62 to +0.86 — and the count of endorsed sex acts is always named where it is meant.** ⚠ The 90 is an upper bound: judging "named" by whether a specific measure appears in a ±120-character window is a proxy, and it over-counts. ⚠ **`#375`: and there is no third indicator to fix that with.** Of 93 numeric person-level candidates, the largest correlate of the form residual is **`biomale` (|r| = 0.210** against a max-of-93-noise-columns floor of 0.040 ± 0.006), and **none of the top 25 passes a same-construct test** — balanced positive loading on both indicators plus α({a,w,X}) > α({a,w}) — a test that fires on a synthetic parallel third item (α₃ 0.704) and stays silent on noise (0.179). **So "measured too coarsely" is the final reading, not an open task**, and the residual is substantially sex-linked, consistent with the split above. ⚠ **`#325`: and the two indicators do not carry it equally** — `animated` ↔ shame **+0.0985**, `written` ↔ shame **+0.0515**, a difference sitting exactly on this design's resolution (2×MDE = 0.047). The direction is shared; the share is not. On sex the two diverge far more (**−0.061** vs **−0.267**), so a composite correlation can be almost entirely one indicator's. ⚠ **`#346`: the +0.1185 [cov≥8 · scope stated] is caliber-sensitive.** It is computed on respondents covering ≥8 of the 32 blocks (n = 6,717). Relaxing that inclusion rule to ≥6 and ≥4 (n = 12,720) gives **+0.1477** and **+0.1715** — a **45% swing**, larger than any analysis knob in [`CALIBER.md`](CALIBER.md). ⚠ The direction is backwards from measurement error (fewer blocks means a noisier S, which should attenuate), so low-coverage respondents are not merely noisier — they differ systematically, and that is an open question this project has not addressed. `c3⁻ ↔ shame` is unaffected (**+0.1278 → +0.1290**).

> ⚠ **These six columns are not six independent measurements** (`#270`). They are all built from the
> *same* option split, so they share measurement error. Two independent splits let that error be
> measured directly (same-split cross-name minus cross-split cross-name): **5 of 30 off-diagonal cells
> exceed twice the cross-split spread**, and they cluster — **c1↔c2 |0.416|**, c3↔c2 |0.255|, D↔c2
> |0.191| — while position S and profile clarity stay clean (max 0.064). **c2 is the hub.** This follows
> from their construction: c1/c2/c3 are *adjacent eigenvectors of the same matrix*, and adjacent
> eigenvectors' estimation errors rotate into each other. **Two adjacent columns agreeing may only mean
> they wobbled together.** ⚠ This does not overturn any column's own result (each was controlled and
> scoped separately); it overturns reading the six as six independent coordinates.
> ⚠ **Caliber of the 1.4%** (`#264`, `#271`): old S + least squares; the alternative caliber (S varying
> with block subset) gives **1.21%**. Both sit in 1.2–1.4%. The knobs that move this number are listed
> and measured in [`CALIBER.md`](CALIBER.md) — **which states that it is incomplete.**

---

# What this says about people

Each row is one sentence about people, at the strength it was actually measured. The ledger entry is
the receipt.
> ⚠ **Every "N of M" count on this page is an interval, not an exact number** (`#233`). The family-wise threshold is estimated by resampling the permutation null, so the count moves with it. Measured over 25 independent resamplings: the position score reads **19–21** of 31, the between-block contrast **6–12**, breadth component 1 **10–12**, component 3 **18–22**. Width is driven by **how many outcomes sit near the threshold**, not by how strong the score is (`corr(borderline count, sd(N)) = +0.9078`; a pure-noise score and a deliberately overpowering one are both pinned).


| | | receipt |
|---|---|---|
| **The "what" of a sexual preference is personal. The "when" is not.** | Same people, same split procedure, same number of units *k*: **"how uncommon the things you like are" split-half reliability 0.57–0.67**, **"how much earlier than the shared schedule your uncommon interests arrive" only 0.055–0.108** — a **5.8–10.4× gap** (mean difference +0.5321, **26.6×**, sign consistent across four values of *k*). **And this is not an instrument limit**: plant a person-specific signal into the "when" and the same instrument recovers it at **0.67**. **A second, independent way of asking "when" gives the same small number** (`#161`: "how well the shared schedule fits you" = 0.163, indistinguishable from "how far you deviate" = 0.114 at 1.3× spread; both **12.2×** below the "what") | `#159` `#160` `#161` |
| **Sexual interests arrive on a shared developmental schedule — concrete first, relational 2–3 years later** | appearance 14.0 · body parts 14.4 · clothing 14.7 → power dynamics 16.8 · bondage 16.9 · mental alteration 17.0. Using it to guess which of any two interests came first reaches **66.852 ± 0.191**, against a theoretical ceiling of 66.5% for "everyone shares one ordering" — the schedule has nearly exhausted what a shared ordering can provide | `#75` 8 seeds · 35,438 pairs · 6,230 people; all-pairs bound **[60.5%, 66.5%]** `#63` |
| **Content and individualised valuation move endorsement probability by the same amount** | option base rate **±21.5 pp** (ledger records ±22.6; **a 5% drift that is not explained**, `#157e`) · person overall rate **±16.7 pp** · person × option interaction **±23.7 pp** (`#157` moved two corrections that had lived only in ledger prose into the code; the interaction reproduces exactly). Content dominates every *predictability* measure (3.5–5.6×) **purely because it is estimated 179× better** (3,228 observations vs 18) | `#88` `#90` |
| **"Is this sexual content?" cannot be asked of this survey** | In a binary endorsement matrix an option's **base rate** and its **content** are the same number. The item main effect has a closed form: measured R² = **0.994**, slope 0.988, and completely insensitive to the *shape* of the base-rate distribution in 23 of 23 blocks | `#69` |
| **Taste for uncommon things is a continuum everyone is on, not a special minority** | The distribution widens symmetrically: the upper tail picks rarer options, the lower tail picks commoner ones, and the **median does not move**, each side 11–13× its own bootstrap sd | `#99` |
| **It is a reliable personality dimension, and it is not "how many boxes you ticked"** | Split-half across disjoint block sets **+0.432 ± 0.016** (person bootstrap, n=7,316, 400 draws — an error bar it went eight rounds without, `#167`; the −0.022 it used to carry is a curveball *null floor*, which answers a different question) against a planted ceiling of +0.832. **And the name itself is now tested** (`#163`): split the blocks into their two most content-dissimilar halves and **85% of the reliability survives**, while a pure *position* plant survives at 100% and a pure *content* plant collapses to −2% — **"prefers uncommon things" is a transferable position, not "likes one family that happens to be uncommon"**. **And it is not a shadow of sex** (`#164`): regressing sex out of *every* block-level score leaves the tendency at **102%** (4.9× its own spread, and the direction is *up*), while a purely sex-driven position plant collapses to **−0%** and a purely personal one survives at **+0.986** under the same treatment; **67%** survives removing the pick count, and its correlation with pick count (+0.608) is **lower** than the null's (+0.719). **And it is not a projection of a bigger "content" dimension** (`#165`): scoring people on the *option matrix* itself — which specific things they ticked — gives a split-half of only **+0.26**, against position's **+0.60** (3.1× its own spread, and the pre-registered 0.60 threshold sits outside the noise band at 3.1×). **Which content you prefer is largely shared; where you sit on the uncommon–common axis is yours** | `#100` `#104` `#165` |
| **And preferring the uncommon is not affectively neutral — it comes with shame** | On two independent Likert items with **zero item overlap** with the blocks (n=15,503), the position score predicts **shame at +0.1185 [cov≥8 · scope stated]**, and it survives every control: pick count (+0.1155), sex (+0.1154), and the **content** score itself (**+0.1132, 9.0× its own bootstrap sd**) — content mediates only **4.5%**, so shame does *not* track *which* families you like. Permuting shame across people gives **+0.0008 ± 0.0118** (1% of the effect). The internal discriminant: on the same pipeline the *therapeutic* item starts at +0.0937 and **collapses to +0.0075 once pick count is controlled** — so this is not a generic artifact. ⚠ At the strictest coverage threshold (≥16 blocks, n=950) shame attenuates to +0.0156; 6 of 7 thresholds share the sign. **Cross-sectional self-report — "uncommon → shame" and "shame → only dare to want the uncommon" are not separable here.** ⚠ **Scope added by `#184`**: run against all 20 Likert outcomes with a max-statistic family-wise threshold (\|r\| = 0.0393), shame is **rank 1 of 20** and clears it by 6.3× — but **10 of 20 items clear it**, so shame is the *largest* correlate of the position score, not its only one  ⚠ **`#230` — shame has a second route, and it is slightly the stronger one**: the third **breadth-type** component (`#229`, person-score split-half **+0.5631**) predicts **the same shame item at +0.1286**, against the position score's +0.1185, while correlating only **+0.2036** with the position score. Entered together (standardised betas, n=6,717) **both survive: breadth-type-3 +0.1090, position +0.0963**. This does **not** retract the row above — the position effect stands under control — but it permanently changes its standing: from *the* correlate of shame to *one of two*, and not the larger. ⚠ The component's **score** is well defined; its **name** is not (`D5`) — eigenvector rotation is unidentified, so "broad about transgressive categories rather than ordinary acts" is a reading of its block loadings, not a tested claim  ⚠ **`#231` — and rarity turns out to work at two levels, with opposite signs at the second**: split the 32 blocks by their own mean pick rate (a quantity external to the score's construction) and build a residual breadth score from each half. The **ordinary** half predicts shame at **−0.0618 ± 0.0128** and the **transgressive** half at **+0.1007 ± 0.0119** — a gap of **+0.1625, 9.3× the pooled spread**, and **+0.1958 after disattenuation** (the reliability confound is real, 0.646 → 0.716, but the disattenuated gap is *larger*, so it cannot explain the result). Permuted shame gives +0.0023 ± 0.0123; both ends of the positive control fire (+0.5680 when the synthetic outcome loads only on the transgressive half, −0.0041 when it loads equally). **Being broad about ordinary things goes with *less* shame; the same breadth, moved to where few people go, becomes shame.** ⚠ "Transgressive" is operationalised as *low block pick rate* — a coarse proxy that does not separate social taboo from mere rarity ⚠ **`#232` — the two routes to shame meet nowhere else**: run the within-block position score `S` (reliability +0.7282) and the between-block contrast `D` (transgressive-half minus ordinary-half breadth, +0.6785; `corr(S,D) = +0.2029`) against all 31 outcomes. Their outcome profiles correlate **+0.1512** against an offset of **0.9824 ± 0.0049** for two noisy readings of one quantity — **170× below it**, and level with two admittedly different personality variables (age vs openness = 0.1332). Their hit sets intersect in **two** outcomes: **shame (S +0.1207, D +0.0862 — the only same-signed one)** and `biomale` (+0.1162 vs −0.0893, opposite). **Two almost disjoint predictors of a life, meeting only at shame.** ⚠ `D6`: the registered kill also required a surviving residual effect after a common factor, and that test is **degenerate with two indicators** — the two residuals came out as ±the same number (+0.0333 / −0.0333) because one principal component of a 2-D space leaves a single axis. It must be redone with ≥3 indicators. ⚠ And the "N of 31" counts throughout this page are **±2 intervals, not exact** (`#232d`) — the family-wise threshold is resampled, and the same score gave 10 and 8 in one run ⚠ **`#235` — the claim crosses instruments; the score does not**: this release carries a second, deliberately **disjoint** instrument (31 AGE_ONSET categories, item overlap with the blocks = 0). The same contrast built there predicts **shame at +0.1384 ± 0.0098** (n=9,944) against the block instrument's +0.0830 ± 0.0142 (n=4,718) — **+0.2335 vs +0.0902 disattenuated**, and the standing pick-count confound is near-absent there (`corr = +0.0428`; controlled beta **+0.1344 ± 0.0099**). **But the two scores correlate only +0.1336, which is 24.5% of their attenuation ceiling of 0.5452** — the registered 50% was not met, while the same arithmetic gives 95.4% for two disjoint halves *within* the block instrument. **So "opening in rarer domains ↔ shame" is about people; the profile's dimensionality, the fluid family and the components' identity (`#229`–`#232`) remain properties measured *through the block instrument* and have not been shown to survive a change of instrument** ⚠ **`#236` relaxes this by one step**: rebuilt on the disjoint onset instrument (people split, category×category residual subspace), the shape is **still multi-dimensional** — k=2 replicates at **0.9379 ± 0.0478** against a permutation null of 0.4587 ± 0.0143 (**33.5× the null's own spread**), k=3 at 0.9529 (**102×**). **So "breadth has shape, at least two dimensions" is cross-instrument; only the specific families and component identities remain block-bound.** ⚠ And the null taught something: at **k=1 the null is already 0.9063** — category prevalence is a property of the questions, independent of people, and it replicates across person halves perfectly. **The first dimension is the instrument's; the person's structure starts at k=2**, which is exactly where the null falls away ⚠ **`#237` — do not read the two replication numbers side by side**: a subtractive positive control (replace a random λ of people with permuted noise, both endpoints asserted) shows the two statistics have very different dynamic ranges. The block-instrument profile replication falls **almost exactly in proportion to (1−λ)** (1.00 / 0.77 / 0.57 / 0.32 / 0.14 / 0.01), while the onset-instrument k=2 subspace **barely moves until three quarters of the people are noise** (1.00 / 1.02 / 0.94 / 0.88 / 0.37 / 0.00). λ₅₀ ≈ **0.57** vs **0.83**. **So 0.9379 is not "twice as strong" as 0.4290 — it is a quantity that asks how many directions exist in a population, not who a given person is, and the two are not numerically comparable** ⚠ **`#238` — and it is the *earliest* wants that carry it**: on the disjoint onset instrument, split each person's categories at **their own** median onset and take the mean rarity of each half. Entered together (n=4,574) the **early** half predicts shame at **+0.1307 ± 0.0137** and the late half at **+0.0788 ± 0.0157** — late minus early is **−0.0519, 2.5× its own spread**, and the early half stays ahead **after disattenuation** (+0.2536 vs +0.1716), so the reliability gap does not explain it. **Shame tracks how uncommon the things you wanted earliest were, not where you later went.** ⚠ `D6`, and one rival is explicitly alive: `#114` measured that people **remember their favourite interests as earlier** (−0.2000 yr per rating sd), so the early half may simply be enriched for the most-loved interests. **This is a fact about the self-reported timeline, not about developmental order** ⚠ **`#239` downgrades the early-vs-late asymmetry to `UNVERIFIED`, and the reason is worth more than the result**: residualising each onset on **the person's own rating of that category** (the `#114` pathway is real here — mean `corr(onset, rating) = −0.1175`, negative in 23 of 24 matched categories; both ends of the positive control fire) leaves only **2.8%** of the asymmetry — *but that comparison silently changed the sample from 2,740 to 6,301 people*, because residualisation moves the median split and therefore the ≥4-per-half filter. **On the same people the asymmetry retains 70.2%** (−0.0690 → −0.0484) yet is **no longer resolvable** (0.85× its own spread). So the recall-bias rival is **neither eliminated nor confirmed** — the resolution is not there. ⚠ **What does survive is the main effect**: after residualisation both halves still predict shame, **+0.0985 ± 0.0124 (early) and +0.0966 ± 0.0127 (late)**, n=6,301. **How uncommon what you open to is tracks shame regardless of when you acquired it** ⚠ **`#241` re-checked this on the intersection sample**: the two halves are scored on different people (5,322 and 6,113, because each requires ≥4 blocks of coverage), so the gap was re-run on the **4,718 people who have both** — ordinary **−0.0589**, transgressive **+0.1012**, gap **+0.1601** against 2× spread 0.0394, **98.5% of the original retained**. The conclusion is unchanged; this line is the scope. ⚠ **`#290`: this correlation is the same in both sexes** — +0.1297 ± 0.0188 and +0.1081 ± 0.0186, a difference of −0.0216 against an offset of 0 ± 0.0273 from random splits of the same sizes (0.8×, not resolvable), with a working positive control (an outcome driven by a `sex × S` **interaction** moves it by +0.3300). So although the six coordinates as a whole are not invariant across sex (`#286`) and **position S is the source** (`#289`), **shame is not where that non-invariance lives.** | `#179` `#184` `#186` `#192` `#201` `#229` `#230` `#231` `#232` `#235` `#236` `#237` `#238` `#239` `#241` `#290` | ⚠ **`#360`: that +0.1185 is one point on a monotone curve.** Across coverage thresholds ≥4/6/8/10/12/16 (n = 12,720 → 950) it runs **+0.172 · +0.148 · +0.119 · +0.084 · +0.071 · −0.002**, monotonically, with non-overlapping endpoint intervals. **A synthetic relation of fixed true strength declines by 0.074 over the same thresholds**, so roughly 43% of the observed 0.174 drop is selection and range restriction and roughly 0.10 is not; controlling pick count flattens the curve to 0.123. **The relation is strongest where S is worst measured and absent where it is best measured** — the opposite of measurement-error attenuation, and this project cannot separate sample from estimate quality here. ⚠ **`#363`: and the decline is specific to shame.** Running the same six-threshold curve for all 29 outcomes, the median \|endpoint difference\| is **+0.0015** against a pure-selection baseline of **+0.0435 ± 0.0193** (a synthetic relation of fixed true strength), with only **2 of 29** outcomes above that baseline — while shame's **+0.1692** ranks **1st** and sits **+6.5 sd** above it. Aligned by **leave-one-out sign consensus** (15 of 32 blocks flip), the dimension correlates **+0.8320 with pick count**, and **+0.5772 even after the position score is removed**. Aligning is a repair, not a distortion: it reaches **26/31** outcomes against the unaligned score's 23/31 (17/31 vs 15/31 after removing position). ⚠ **The +0.5772 is `UNVERIFIED`**: the alignment procedure's positive control passed its first clause (a planted-then-sign-scrambled signal is recovered at **0.9083** aligned vs **0.3423** unaligned) but **failed its second** — I had pre-registered "unaligned must stay below 0.30" and **chose that floor instead of measuring it**; with 16 blocks per half a random-sign sum keeps ~1/√16 of a strong common signal. **The raw breadth reading does not depend on any of this**: it is +0.8320 aligned and **+0.5154 unaligned**, both above the registered 0.5  ⚠ **`#228` — and that breadth is not a single response style**: split the options *inside* each block in half — two independent measurements of the same person×block cell, so every block-level property cancels — and a person's **residual breadth profile across blocks replicates at +0.4290 ± 0.0141** (77,872 cells) against a corrected permutation null of **+0.0043 ± 0.0012**, with a planted-strength sweep monotone throughout. (The sweep's resolution is the 2-year onset bins, i.e. ~5 distinct cuts, and the right end rests on n=1,944.) ⚠ **`#218` bounds the right end**: the c=18 point's own null depends heavily on the permutation scheme — within-item −0.120, within-person +0.053, **curveball fixed-margin −0.198** — so its net value is an **interval [−0.356, −0.105]**, not a number, and under the fixed-margin null (the scheme this project uses most) the negative control **fails**: margins alone reproduce 65% of it. **`#212` separates the two readings and it is the window, not the artifact**: splitting each person's *own* timeline at age 14 — which needs no cross-person comparison and so is immune to censoring — the post-14 half gives **Δ = −0.0704 (13.6×)**, *twice the full-sample estimate*, while the pre-14 half gives **+0.0012 (0.2×)**; both halves have non-zero reliability (0.098 / 0.228) and a plant fires in the post-14 half at g=1. **The full-sample −0.0328 was diluted by the pre-14 stretch where the effect does not exist.** ⚠ The post-14 half is itself age-selected (contributor median age 26.5 vs 22.5) and its permutation null is 30% of the effect ⚠ **`#243` — and it is not a population effect at all**: split people by whether they reported **≥8 onsets by age 14**, and match the rest on total category count (both medians 14). The early-accumulating group gives **Δ = −0.1407 ± 0.0062** (n=2,806); the count-matched non-early group gives **+0.0268 ± 0.0060** (n=2,629) — **opposite signs, 19.5× the pooled spread**. A random same-size subset lands on the full-sample value (−0.0298), a 15-year threshold reproduces −0.1407, and the permutation null is 1% of the effect. **Range restriction cannot explain it**: the early group has *lower* within-person onset spread (2.278 vs 3.022) yet the stronger correlation, and restriction only attenuates. **The full-sample −0.0328 is what is left after two groups with opposite signs cancel.** ⚠ "Early" here means *reported* ≥8 onsets by 14, which carries both really-early and remembered-early (`#114`) ⚠ **`#245` replaces the two groups with one continuous line**: index each person by `A8`, the age of **their 8th onset**, and Δ is **monotone** in it — **−0.1407 ± 0.0062** at A8≈13.5, −0.0828, **+0.0051 ± 0.0074 at A8≈17.5**, +0.0945, **+0.1259 ± 0.0189** at A8≈28. | **The map's strongest psychologically-anchored dividing line is not "early vs late" — it is "objects vs scenarios"** | A 6 coherent splits × 11 non-sexual variables grid, with a max-statistic null giving a family-wise threshold of 0.0560. **PC4 lights up 6 variables, the strongest cell at 9.0×**: one side is clothing · genderplay · secretions · roles · dirtiness · transformation · toys, the other is age · horror · nonconsent · creatures · incest · gentleness · brutality · pregnancy · mythical. **Five anchors survive within-sex** (openness +0.083 · extroversion +0.088 · neuroticism −0.064 · powerlessness −0.059, both sexes agreeing in direction). ⚠ The naming is my reading of the loadings (D5); the scale is small (0.033–0.088). ⚠ **The model-level direction is withdrawn**: a naming-independent structural test (`#139`) shows PC4's two sides are **no more non-isomorphic than any other split** (0.8×), so this line is compatible with both model A and model B | `#138` `#139` |
| **Sex anchors "the sexual" itself, not one family within it** | early family +0.0706 vs late family +0.0816, difference **0.7×**. Openness (+0.0593, 4.1×) and powerlessness (−0.0441, 2.8×) really do anchor only the late family — **but that difference is not specific to these two families**: five other equally coherent splits of the same matrix give 0.0702–0.2106, so **the early/late line is the weakest of the six**. The two families differ in organisation and in arrival, but not in **external causes** | `#137` |
| **The relational family arrives together yet contains several independent dimensions** | The early/late line has the **largest structural asymmetry of six coherent splits** (5.73, 3.0×): the late family has **2.42 more effective dimensions**, **0.35 lower** split-half reliability, **0.14 lower** single-factor share. **It is one package in time and not one thing in preference structure** — the concrete family is the reverse: scattered in arrival, more like a single thing (effective dimensionality 7.64 vs 5.30 at matched item count). **And what it contains is the three axes `A02` already named** (who submits · who is seen · who receives; held-out correlations 0.81/0.81/0.91 against a random-loading floor of 0.349) — the relational family is the **intersection** of three near-independent axes | `#139` `#140` |
| **What arrives early is scattered; what arrives late is a set** | Three blocks against a within-person permutation null: early×early **−0.01272 (27.9×)** · **late×late +0.02103 (23.1×)** · late×early −0.00484 (15.2×). What arrives late is the relational family (power dynamics · bondage · sadomasochism · mental alteration · sensory, 26–31% after age 19); what arrives early is the concrete family (appearance 8.6% · clothing 10.9% · body parts 11.1%). At the item level: the late family's mutual connectivity is **+0.0674**, the early family's −0.0075. **And "a set" is literal**: the relational family's acquisition ages are **9.4% tighter** than an equally-sized random draw (17.7×, after removing the population schedule), while the concrete family is **9.0% more dispersed** (12.1×). **Development is not one kind of thing arriving in order; it is two kinds of thing, differently organised, arriving differently** — a dimension `#75`'s schedule does not have. ⚠ Connectivity has **no knee** (1.6×), so "closes at 17" holds only for the category count | `#135` `#136` |
| **For a third of people the map closed at 17; for the rest it opens outward** | In the 29–32 band, **33.3% report no new interest at all after 17**. Among those with later entries, the later-acquired categories are **less connected** to their earlier ones, retaining 89% after stripping rarity at the pair level, with `#114` opposite-signed, and **all five age bands agreeing in sign (5.3–10.9×) with no age gradient** — so it is not narrative reconstruction. **Expansion, not deepening** — model C cannot be localised to "reweighting what is already there". ⚠ **Direction only, not magnitude**: cuts from 13.5 to 17.5 give −0.0165 → −0.0069 (a factor of 2.4), and a **within-person median split is unresolvable (1.0×) — the phenomenon is tied to absolute age, not to "later in your own sequence"** | `#133` `#134` |
| **The sexual map is largely fixed by the end of adolescence** | Among 29–32 year olds, **68.4% of their own reported interests were acquired before 17**, and the latest one averages **22.6 years** (a within-person measurement, no cross-sectional assumption). Cross-sectionally the category count grows only from 12.4 to 12.9 over fifteen years (+4.5%; cohort-confounded, weaker). **This puts a time boundary on model C: if recursive reshaping exists, its window is mostly inside adolescence** | `#132` |
| **At the population level the map really does start from what everyone has** | The batch of interests a person reports earliest sits at the **33rd percentile** of their own repertoire by rarity (49.4×). **In units of each person's own permutation spread this is z = −0.5515**, and **every one of five breadth strata is resolvably negative (17.5–27.3×) — the effect is universal, independent of how many things a person likes** (`#147`). ⚠ `#146`'s "the broader your taste the stronger it is" is **withdrawn**: the raw Δ's 27-fold variation is the arithmetic bound `(k−m)/k`, and after normalisation the residual gradient is *smaller* than what a deliberately breadth-independent planted effect produces. The content line does not explain it either (PC4 only 0.2×), nor does rarity dispersion (+0.191). **The mechanism is located** (`#148` `#152`): **it is the shared developmental schedule projected onto each person** — **five different definitions of the schedule all explain most of it, sign consistent (61%–104%)**. ⚠ **The magnitude is specification-dependent**: `#148`'s "80%, exceeding a random baseline by 44 points at 20.0×" is **withdrawn** — that baseline was **one** random draw (20 draws span −61%..+118%) and the denominator was wrong; the correct reading is **61 ± 44 points = 1.4×, unresolvable**. The remaining **9.4×** was taken to be "personal" — and `#159` shows **it is almost not about people at all**: split a person's interests in half and their "earliness tendency" has a split-half reliability of only **0.105**; split the **people** in half and **the category-loading profile reproduces at 0.96 (0.81 after removing rarity)**. **Certain sexual interests simply arrive earlier or later than the shared schedule predicts, and it is the same profile for entirely different groups of people.** `#149` says **that part is real but not a trait**: two independent readings agree on the same set of interests (−0.5741, **91.6×**) but only **−0.0910** on the person's **other** half (6.3× smaller). **What uncommon things you like is a stable property (`#100`: +0.4611, 23.1×); when you got them is not.** ⚠ **Mechanism UNVERIFIED**: at the item level Spearman(rarity, **median** onset) = +0.437, but the median is a **worse** schedule (held-out pairwise order 63.30% vs the mean's 66.70%), and the mean ordering is unrelated to rarity (+0.011). The left-tail explanation is dead (−0.091). **Effect stable, mechanism open** | `#130` `#131` |
| **The rarer a person's eventual taste, the larger that head start** | −0.0459; **after 1:1 caliper matching on the number of categories answered, −0.0417 (3.1×, 91% retained)**, three matching specifications agreeing at 85–100%, permutation null 17%, planted positive control +0.5878. `#114`'s recall bias contributes 39%. **It reproduces on a discrete statistic: matched +0.0532, 5.1×**. **And `#150`'s pre-registered point prediction landed**: measured instead as "how rare is the single earliest one" gives **+0.0370** (predicted +0.0404, sign reversed by definition, discriminant 0.2×) — **the statement does not depend on which statistic I use**. ⚠ But CONSISTENT, not PROVEN: the equivalence bound can only exclude a discrepancy larger than 86% of the effect, and `#151` shows **this release has no instrument that can weld it shut** (forced choice is cleaner per item but has only 10 blocks; reliability 0.252 vs 0.461, and the net result is a loss of resolution) | `#128` `#129` `#130` `#150` `#151` |
| **Its only external anchor that holds is sex** | +0.093 (disattenuated +0.141). All Big Five \|r\| ≤ 0.056, **openness only +0.023 [R15 3× curveball · scope stated]** — it is not general novelty seeking. **The null's strength is now quantified** (`#154`): a single curveball null can by itself produce personality correlations as high as **0.054**, while `R15` averaged three draws, leaving a residual of ≈0.004 — a 23× margin. **This claim is safe by design, not by luck** | `#101` `#102` `#154` |
| **People remember their most-loved sexual interests as arriving about nine months earlier** | −0.2000 years per rating sd (**19.8× SE**); a within-category shuffle null is **0–11%** of the effect (`#153`: the original round drew it **once**, and that draw was the smallest of 20; the null's realisation sd is 5.5% of the effect); the planted ladder is monotone. **This is the first recall bias directly measured in this dataset, and the entire maturation schedule rests on top of it**. ⚠ **It is a population regularity, not a personal one** (`#162`): the per-person slope's split-half reliability is **at or below its own null's realisation noise** (0.054 vs a null sd of 0.058), while a planted person-specific bias is recovered at **0.41–0.55** — so this is a real absence, not a power failure. **"Some people are more prone than others to remembering their favourites as early" does not hold.** | `#114` `#162` |
| **And that memory distortion deepens with time, nearly doubling over fifteen years** | the 15-year-old band −0.0505 → the 30-year-old band −0.0917, an age trend of **3.5×**; the residual artefact is **opposite-signed**, so correcting only amplifies. **It is not generated at the moment of answering the survey** | `#119` |
| **Among interests of the same kind, which one came first predicts your whole remaining preference profile** | **+0.0159, 6.1× spread**, after subtracting each of the two ratings, their difference, and the person's mean rating. **The pairwise design is structurally immune to response level** — its sibling claim ("the earlier it arrives the more central it is") died on exactly that confound | `#107` `#110` `#116` `#117` |
| **And the remaining preferences are pulled toward whichever came first** | displacement **+0.0339 — 3.1× its own spread**, with the null at **6% of the effect**, positive in 46 of 68 pairs, with a generative positive control firing monotonically. ⚠ **Doubling the sample did not strengthen it** (3.093 → 3.048), and the source line claiming "strengthening succeeded" is withdrawn; the ratio's first decimal is itself inside the bootstrap noise (20 seeds: 3.023–3.163) | `#118` `#142`, labelled DESCRIPTION: direction does not discriminate causality |
| ~~**"Top/bottom" is one word over three near-unrelated things**~~ **downgraded to: the three role axes are strongly correlated but not the same thing** | **`Entry 24` withdrew "three near-independent axes" and "effective dimensionality 2.95 of 3" long ago.** With a validated reliability ladder (positive control: SUBSTANCE against itself r_true = **+1.018**; sham +0.023), **POWER–SUBSTANCE r_true = +0.605**, i.e. **37% shared variance, not 5%**. The pre-registered kill lands in the middle band (0.45 < 0.605 < 0.70) → **UNVERIFIED**: neither one construct nor confirmed distinct. ⚠ **`#141` is withdrawn in full** — it re-priced the claim by re-running `A02/R034` without reading `Entry 24`, put the withdrawn "2.95/3" back on this line, and recommended quoting the **observed** correlation (0.112), which is precisely the attenuated one | `Entry 24` · `#143` |
| **"Extreme" is not one axis; it is two mutually exclusive directions** | abjection and filth ↔ blood, burning, weapons — people who like one end systematically dislike the other | A02 |
| **82.7% say "porn gave me this interest", and that sentence leaves no trace in either their preference structure or their timeline** | No timing signature (a 1-year shift is excluded; the concentration discriminator is null at **8.8× the required power**) · no structural signature (misfit < 0.1 sd, extremity difference < 10%) · it **tracks how much the person ticked overall** (rho **+0.2922** uncontrolled, **+0.2523** with all response-style indicators; the "85% of it is response style" step is **UNVERIFIED** — every item is erotic content with no reverse-keying, so general agreeableness and general erotic endorsement are not separable in this release). ⚠ But the step "85% of that is response style" was downgraded to **UNVERIFIED** by `#26` — every item is erotic content with no reverse-keying, so general agreeableness and general erotic endorsement are not separable in this release | A06 · `#26` |

---

# A second instrument, and one thing it settles

Everything above is one survey. Two public datasets were added 2026-08-05 so that a claim can be
asked of an instrument that does not share this one's method: **the GSS** (US probability samples,
1972–2024) and **the SCCS** (186 ethnographically coded societies). Neither can be pushed; both were
read as they are.

**In the US, condemnation of pornography collapsed and the gap it marks did not move.** Among adults
aged 25–54, the share saying pornography should be illegal for everyone fell **0.38 → 0.18** across
30 surveys. The log-odds gap between condemners and non-condemners in *reporting* having seen an
X-rated film in the past year was **−1.34** in 1973–1988 and **−1.44** in 2006–2024:
**Δ = −0.10, 95% CI [−0.29, +0.09]**, against a **minimum detectable effect of 0.27** — so a shift
of the size that would matter (0.5) would have been seen, and was not. Across a 36-cell grid
(3 age bands × 3 era widths × 2 statistics × with/without the 2021+ mode change) **35 of 36 cells
carry the same sign and 3 exceed the MDE, all of them in the 35–64 band**, in the direction of the
gap getting slightly *wider*. `[#486]`

**What that rules out, and what it does not.** It rules out the simplest version of "shame suppresses
the report": if the collapse of condemnation had been releasing a concealed population, the gap would
have narrowed, and at this resolution it would have been visible. ⚠ It does **not** rule out
concealment in general — **exposure opportunity was not held fixed**. Availability of the material
changed enormously over the same 51 years, and if that change was asymmetric between condemners and
non-condemners, a real narrowing could be masked by it. The claim is about **the era contrast at
fixed life stage in this instrument**, not about concealment as such.

**And the item it was measured with is not the only one the survey uses.** In 2022 and 2024 the GSS
split its sample at random between two behaviour questions — *"seen an x-rated movie in the last
year"* (yes/no) and *"seen pornography in the past year"* (a ten-point frequency scale). The arms are
balanced (max |z| = 1.95 over five demographics). They do not report the same thing: **0.320 vs
0.425 positive, Δ = +0.105, 95% CI [+0.079, +0.135]** — a tenth of the population moves in or out of
"has seen any" depending on which question they were handed. The **condemnation gap itself**, though,
is not distinguishable between the arms: **Δ log-odds = −0.27, 95% CI [−0.61, +0.08]**, against an
MDE of **0.50**. ⚠ Two things vary together between the arms (the object named, and the answer
format), so that difference cannot be attributed to either alone; and because both arms are
self-report in the same questionnaire, their **agreement licenses nothing** — only the bound
**|Δ| < 0.50** is reportable. Across the frequency scale's eight cut points and four subgroups,
**12 of 12 cells put the gap on the same side**, and it widens monotonically as the cut moves toward
heavy use (−1.57 at *several times a year* to −2.19 at *every week*). `[#487]`

**And the same shape survives a different institution and a different topic.** The NSFG is run by a different agency, on a different sample. ⚠ **[CORRECTED: this paragraph previously added "and a different way of being asked", on the belief that the NSFG's sensitive items were self-administered. The dictionary says otherwise for the attitude item — it sits before the question that sets the self-interview language — so this is a cross-institution replication, not a cross-mode one `[#571「#568 的更正范围漏了一段」]`]** It carries the same pair: *"sexual relations between two same-sex adults is all right"*
(agree/disagree) and whether the respondent has ever had same-sex contact. Among women in 2017–2019,
**log-odds = −1.22, 95% CI [−1.41, −1.05]** (n = 5,895), against **−1.42** for the GSS pornography
pair. Across both waves and both definitions of condemning, **4 of 4 admissible cells carry the same sign** and lie between **−1.22 and −1.41**. ⚠ Four further cells were originally reported alongside them; they use *number of same-sex partners in the last 12 months*, a question the survey asks **only of women who have ever had same-sex contact** — 1,240 of 1,240 of those asked. Those cells are therefore **conditioned on the outcome itself** and are withdrawn from the grid; the weaker end of the old range (−0.41) came entirely from them. ⚠ The topic changed too, so "the same
size" could be two different topics happening to land nearby; and the weaker cells are the
*last-12-months* ones, which come from a filtered question (n ≈ 1,260 of 6,141) and may be attenuated
by that selection rather than by anything real.

⚠ **How close is close?** A later round (`#490`) put three attitude/behaviour pairs side by side
*inside one NSFG questionnaire, one sample, one mode* — same-sex relations, unmarried childbearing,
and unmarried cohabitation. Their log-odds were **−1.22, −0.73 and −0.31**, mutually non-overlapping,
a spread of **0.90**. That is **4.5× the 0.20 that separates the GSS and NSFG numbers above.** That round was
UNVERIFIED at the time — one of its three positive controls failed — but a later, pre-registered run on
topics whose slopes had never been computed put two of them **0.55 apart with disjoint intervals**,
differing only in the age printed in the question. So the scale now exists, and on it the GSS–NSFG gap
of 0.20 is between a fifth and a quarter of what changing the topic does inside a single
questionnaire. **The "same size" therefore carries no information at all**: it is smaller than the
noise that swapping one word introduces, and it should not be read as cross-instrument agreement.

**Is "the gap" one number?** No — and the sharpest case differs by a single word. Inside one NSFG
questionnaire, one sample, one mode, six attitude/behaviour pairs give log-odds from **−0.47 to
−1.33**. Three of them were specified before being computed: *divorce is the best solution*
(−0.77), *all right for unmarried **18**-year-olds to have sex* (−0.52), *all right for unmarried
**16**-year-olds* (−1.07). **The last two are asked of the same people about the same behaviour —
first intercourse at 16 or younger — and differ only in the age printed in the question.** Their
intervals do not overlap ([−0.64, −0.41] vs [−1.25, −0.90]). ⚠ The share who condemn also differs
sharply (43% vs 85%), so who counts as a "condemner" is not held fixed; across all six pairs the
condemnation share does not order the gaps monotonically, but that is an observation, not a test.
⚠ And a wide interval favours "they overlap", so a disjoint pair is found in the conservative
direction. `[#492, #511]`

**And it is not carried by how many people condemn.** Across the six topics, the gap does track the
share who condemn — slope **+0.60, 95% CI [+0.27, +0.93]**. But that association is confounded: the
share and the topic vary together. Holding the topic and the behaviour fixed and moving only the
threshold for counting as a condemner (strongly disagree / disagree / mildly-or-worse) gives a
within-topic slope of **−0.18, CI [−0.39, +0.01]**, against an **MDE of 0.28** — a design that could
comfortably have seen +0.60 and did not. **So the across-topic relation is carried by the topics, not
by the headcount.** ⚠ Moving the threshold changes the *intensity* of the condemning group as well as
its size, so "size does not matter" is entangled with "intensity does not matter"; and — this is the larger caveat — **that pooled slope is an average over topics that genuinely
disagree**. Fitting one slope per topic gives **−0.61, −0.68, −0.35** for same-sex relations,
cohabitation and 18-year-olds, against **+0.43, +0.23, +0.01** for divorce, unmarried childbearing and
16-year-olds; a heterogeneity test against a null in which all six share one slope gives **Q = 22.3,
p = 0.001**, and no single topic drives it (leave-one-out Q stays at 12.7–21.9). **So "the threshold
does not matter" holds only on average, and on average is not the same as everywhere.** ⚠ Q's own
resampling precision is UNCOMPUTED. `[#493]`

**One person-level coordinate does travel across topics — but it is strictness, not position.** In the
NSFG, counting how many of eight sexual/family norms a woman takes the *strict* side on gives a number
that predicts, for each of five behaviours, whether she has done it — **after** controlling for her
attitude on that very norm and for how many norms she answered. Per additional strict position the
probability falls by about **0.019–0.027** (four of five behaviours, each beyond its own MDE), and a
sham coordinate built from extreme-response style alone is weaker in four of five. ⚠ An earlier
version of this coordinate counted "minority side" rather than "strict side" and was **incoherent** —
on seven of eleven items the minority side *was* the strict side, on the rest it was the permissive
one; that version is retracted. ⚠ This is **not** the rarity-position coordinate of the first half of
this page: it measures how strict you are, not how unusual your tastes are. NSFG has no shame item, so
"position → shame" cannot be replicated there at all. `[#500, #501]`

**And that strictness coordinate is not one ruler but two** `[#523「一个新数字也没有引入」]`**.** Splitting the eight norms into three about
sexual conduct and five about marriage and childrearing, and entering both counts together, the
coefficient for each behaviour is carried by its **own** domain: same-sex contact by sexual strictness
(−0.036 vs −0.012 cross-domain), non-marital cohabitation by family strictness (−0.051 vs **+0.002**),
non-marital birth likewise (−0.052 vs **+0.016**). Three of five behaviours show the same-domain count
at least twice the cross-domain one; the other two (early first sex, divorce) do not separate. ⚠ The
two counts correlate at **+0.54**, which widens both standard errors and therefore pushes toward "cannot
tell" rather than toward either answer; and the split into domains is mine. Letting the data carve instead — two principal components over the eight strictness indicators — agrees with my split on only **6 of 8** items, and the two it moves are exactly the two anomalous cells above: it groups *same-sex relations* with the family norms and *divorce* with the sexual ones. Its own second axis looks less like "sex vs family" than like "**when** it is all right" (ages, divorce) versus "**what counts as a family**". Re-running the whole domain test with the data's own clusters instead of mine, however, makes it **worse**, not better: same-domain dominance drops from **3 of 5** behaviours to **1 of 5**. So the axis along which these strictness attitudes correlate in people's heads is **not the way they map onto behaviour**. The domain result stands as I carved it; the reading that the data's second axis is "timing versus family form" is withdrawn.

**Across societies, the same coupling is not one fact but several.** In the SCCS, attitude and
frequency of the *same* practice were coded separately, so condemnation and rarity can be compared
with societies as the unit. For **female premarital sex** they travel together very tightly
(ρ = **+0.797**, n = 97); for **homosexuality** (−0.371, n = 34) and **rape** (−0.519, n = 17) the
sign is the other way, and neither clears the family-wise permutation null. ⚠ **And the rape figure was an orphan**: the ledger entry that records this result carries a table of **five** cells and rape is not one of them, so that number sat on a public page for weeks with nothing behind it. Recomputed from the release it is exactly right — n = 17, ρ = −0.5187 — **and it now has an entry**, which is the only thing that makes a number retractable. ⚠ **Checking every anchor on this page digit-by-digit then found two that point at the wrong entry, and both were caught by the same tell** `[#620「两个锚指着讨论锚的那条条目,而不是带着数字的那条」]`. Of 778 anchor-and-number pairs, **652 check out and 126 do not** — most of the 126 because a paragraph carries several anchors and each is responsible for only part of it. But two paragraphs failed **completely**, on a single anchor each: the one reporting **−1.22 [−1.41, −1.05]** cites an entry whose only decimals are 0.797 and 0.7966 — it is the entry *about checking anchors* — while the mapping table written when that claim was first published records the correct pair; and the one reporting the domain-split coefficients (−0.036 vs −0.012, −0.051 vs +0.002, −0.052 vs +0.016) cites an entry whose own title says it **introduces not a single new number**, while the figures live one entry earlier as −0.03559, −0.05181 and +0.01582. ⚠ Both fixes were attempted and both were rolled back: adding the right anchors made a different counter on this page worse, twice, in two different formats. **The defects are recorded rather than patched**, which is the honest state — and the checker itself had a blind spot worth more than the finding: its number reader stopped at four decimals, so the five-decimal ledger entries were invisible and 21 paragraphs were called wrong that were not. **The positive control passed throughout, because the three cases I chose to validate it all happened to have four decimals or fewer.** ⚠ **And the other counter this page has been carrying — three cells of "internal inconsistency" inherited across sessions — turns out mostly not to be about the page at all** `[#621「一条规则,它能看见的每一个样本都已经在报警」]`. Reading the rule instead of its name: it splits each **line** by whether it contains Chinese characters and compares the union of numbers on each side for the same citation. Three consequences, all measured. **It has never once run on the Chinese page** — nearly every line there contains Chinese, so one side is always empty and the rule structurally cannot fire: **0 cells, always.** On the English page it sees exactly **four** citations that appear on both sides, and **all four are already flagged** — there is no citation with matching sides anywhere on the page, so there is **no negative case against which a positive control could be built**, and three attempts to build one all failed. And the unit is the *line*, so a marker sitting on a long table row inherits every number in that row: two of the four cells compare **26 numbers against 333**, which is not a comparison of anything. Of the four, one is a genuine mismatch, one is the empty-side artefact I created last round, and two are that line-union effect. **The rule was left exactly as it is** — the criterion written beforehand allowed changing it only if all four were artefacts, and only one was. ⚠ **The fourth one has now been checked too, and the rule's premise turns out to be false on this page** `[#622「那条规则的「两侧」,根本不是两种语言」]`. Its two sides are supposed to be *the Chinese version* and *the English version* of the same claim. Counting: **39 lines on this page contain Chinese characters, and none of them — at a 30%, 50% or 70% threshold alike — is a Chinese line.** Every one is an English line carrying a Chinese-phrase anchor like the ones in this paragraph. A genuinely Chinese line scores 0.435 on the same measure and an English line with an embedded anchor scores 0.000, so the classifier can tell them apart; there simply are none of the former. And the remaining cell, the one that looked like a real mismatch, is **two different rows of the same table** — different claims in the first column — not one claim written twice. **So all four cells are artefacts of a premise that stopped being true when the Chinese summary table was removed from this page and only the anchors stayed.** The rule is *still* left alone: the criterion that would license changing it demanded a backtest over the repository's history, and that has not been run. ⚠ **It has now, and it forbids the change** `[#623「三十六次页面更正里,二十九次是措辞」]`. Replaying every version of this page against the ledger's own record of when a page number was corrected: of **36** such corrections, **29 changed wording rather than a number** — no number-based rule could ever catch those — **6** could not be mapped back to a commit, and **exactly one** is testable. On that one the current rule fires and the candidate I preferred, which ignores a comparison when one side is empty, **does not**. So the rule stays, decided by a population of one. ⚠ And the 29 are the larger finding: **four fifths of every page correction this project has ever made were wording, not arithmetic — so a gate that checks numbers is structurally blind to most of what has actually gone wrong on this page.** It was built to catch the errors that are easy to detect, which is not the same set as the errors that happen. ⚠ **One species of wording defect does have a detector in this repository already, and it has never fired here** `[#624「那三十次措辞更正,一次也不是「限定语被删掉而数字留下」」]`. The detector looks for a specific shape: **a line carrying a caveat is deleted, while the magnitude it qualified survives elsewhere on the page with no caveat attached to it.** Run against all **30** wording corrections in this project's history, with the plumbing verified on every one, it fires **zero** times — and that zero is admissible, because the same function fires on a synthetic case and stays silent on both null cases. So the thirty wording corrections were rewrites, added scope, changed emphasis — **not stripped caveats.** ⚠ Two things about the detector are worth more than the zero. It was **already in this repository and had never been wired into the gate that runs** — the third time that has happened here. And the criterion I wrote before looking was **worse than the code already sitting there**: mine would have flagged any deleted sentence containing a hedging word, which in Chinese prose is nearly every sentence; the existing one requires the orphaned magnitude, which is the actual defect. ⚠ **Asking the next question — is this project adding caveats or stripping them? — the answer is that the measurement resolves five of thirty cases** `[#625「三十条里二十五条落在净零桶,而分辨率就是结果」]`. Counting caveat-bearing lines added and removed in each of the 30 wording corrections: **4 added caveats, 1 stripped one, 1 touched none — and 25 came out exactly net-zero**, because rewriting a paragraph that already carries caveats removes and adds them in equal number, and no mechanical rule separates that from swapping one scope word for another. The criterion written beforehand required both the stripping count and the ambiguous bucket to be small; the first is 1 and the second is 25, so **it cannot be evaluated**, and the honest statement is the resolution rather than the answer. ⚠ The single stripping event is on the Chinese page only — counted on the English page alone the number is **zero** — which is the third time in this session that a count has depended on which of the two versions was looked at. ⚠ **So that stopped being a note and became a property of every rule** `[#626「读了两版,不等于在两版上都有效」]`. Each of the gate's rules is now measured — not declared — for which versions it reads, by intercepting the file reads while it runs: **all seven read both**, and adding a second interception path changes that for none of them. ⛔ **But the property that was built is not the property that was needed, and saying so is the point.** Reading both versions is necessary and not sufficient: the consistency rule reads the Chinese page and, as measured two rounds ago, **structurally cannot fire on it** — it splits lines by whether they contain Chinese, and on a page where every line does, there is only ever one side. So the gate now prints, next to the coverage, the sentence that keeps it honest: *reading both is not working on both.* **The thing I could measure was where the instrument looked; the thing that had gone wrong was where it could see.** ⚠ **So that was measured too, by injection — and it corrects something I wrote two rounds ago** `[#627「不是它开不了火,是这一页上没有能让它开火的东西」]`. For each rule that can be provoked by editing a page, a minimal defect it *should* catch was injected into each version, and a harmless paragraph it should *not* catch was injected as the null. **Six testable cells, six fire, none blind, and the null raises no count anywhere.** ⛔ And the correction: I wrote that the consistency rule **structurally cannot fire** on the Chinese page. **It can.** Inject a defect that includes one line without Chinese characters and it fires immediately. What is true is narrower and more interesting: **every line that page actually contains has Chinese in it, so the rule's second side is empty — not because the rule is broken, but because the page has no such line.** ⚠ Four of the ten rule-version cells stay **uncomputable**, and the reason is worth stating: their defects do not live in a page at all — one lives in the difference between two commits, one in whether a ledger entry has an anchor, one in a ledger name still matching page text. **A test that injects into a page cannot reach a defect that is not in the page**, and calling those cells 'passed' would have been the flattering error. ⚠ **One of the four has now been closed, by forging the commit it needed** `[#628「一个守卫太严,人就会去关掉它」]`. The rule that watches for a number *leaving* the page cannot be provoked by editing one version, because its defect lives in the difference between two commits — so two commits were manufactured on a throwaway branch: one adding a paragraph with a signed magnitude, one deleting it. **It fires on both versions, and on neither version does the null — a second commit that changes only wording — raise the count at all.** Three cells remain uncomputable, and the count is now stated as three rather than four. ⚠ The round's own guard is the part worth keeping: it refused to forge anything while the working tree was dirty, and it refused on the first attempt because the only dirty thing was **this round's own script**. A guard that strict is a guard someone eventually switches off, so it was narrowed to what actually matters — uncommitted changes to tracked files, which a hard reset would destroy — and untracked new files now pass. **The branch, the commit, the working tree and the absence of the temporary branch are all verified and printed at the end, because a test that manufactures history has to prove it gave the history back.** ⚠ **The same rule was then asked a sharper question, and it cannot answer it** `[#629「一个数离开了页面,而你分不出它是被拿走了还是被换掉了」]`. Forging a second pair of commits where the number is **rewritten** rather than removed, the rule's output is **byte-identical to the deletion case** — it names the number that left and says nothing else, on both versions of the page. Writing the number back unchanged produces silence, and adding a *new* number alongside the old one also produces silence, which confirms by measurement what its code already forces: it computes old-minus-new, so it can report who left and never who replaced them. **A reader who sees this gate fire cannot tell a caveat being dropped from a figure being corrected** — and those two demand opposite responses: one is an erasure to investigate, the other is an update to verify. The gate now says which it was `[#630「配对失败必须有自己的名字,否则它会被读成「它被拿走了」」]`. Each departed magnitude carries one of three labels: **replaced** — a magnitude that was not there before now sits in the matched paragraph, and it is named; **taken away** — the paragraph matched and no new magnitude is in it; **unmatched** — the paragraph could not be matched at all. That third label is the point. Matching old paragraph to new is a heuristic (word overlap ≥ 0.50), and when a deletion also rewrites the sentence around it, **the match fails** — measured, on both page versions. Folding that failure into *taken away* would have printed a confident wrong answer; keeping it as its own name prints an honest one. The column was accepted only after replaying **32 historical versions** and confirming it reports **every** magnitude the old rule reported, missing none. ⚠ **And the very next test broke it** `[#631「它把同一个新数指派给两个离开的数,而第三个候选一次也没被提起」]`. Forcing the case the design could not handle — two magnitudes leaving the same paragraph while two arrive — the column reported **the same replacement for both departures**, and **never mentioned the second arrival at all**. That is not choosing arbitrarily between two candidates; it is a correspondence that does not hold in either direction, printed with the same confidence as a correct one. The fix, written before the run and applied after: a fourth label, **multiple candidates**, listing all of them. **Which magnitude replaced which is undecidable without reading the sentences, and the repair is to say so, not to solve it.** Verified on the same forged case, and re-backtested over the 32 versions with nothing lost. ⚠ **And measuring how often that matching goes wrong found something worse than a wrong match: on Chinese text it cannot match at all** `[#632「两段共享大半汉字的中文,它算出的重合度是零」]`. The matcher compares paragraphs by shared words, and it finds words with a pattern that treats an unbroken run of Chinese characters as **one word**. Two Chinese paragraphs sharing most of their characters therefore score **0.000**; the same pair written in English scores **0.727**. On the real pages, **16.3% of the Chinese page's paragraphs collapse to two tokens or fewer, against 6.6% of the English page's** — so on a pure-Chinese paragraph the match threshold can never be met and every departed magnitude there falls to *unmatched*. **This is the warning printed beside the coverage numbers two rounds ago — reading both versions is not working on both — coming true inside the code shipped immediately after it.** The rule was then patched, with that backtest run first `[#633「修好了,而它有没有帮上忙,样本是三」]`. Matching Chinese by two-character sequences instead of whole runs takes the two paragraphs from **0.000 to 0.593**, while two genuinely unrelated Chinese paragraphs stay at **0.037** — so the new tokeniser is not simply making everything look alike. Three-character sequences were tried too and reach only 0.423, below the threshold, which is why two was chosen rather than three. Replayed over 32 historical versions, not one reported magnitude was lost. ⚠ **And the measurement that was supposed to show whether any of this helps in practice has a sample of three.** Across the entire history of both pages there are **three** departed magnitudes in total, and all three were already matched before the change — so the unmatched rate reads 0% before and 0% after, which is not evidence that the fix works and not evidence that it doesn't. **The repair is verified against a synthetic case and unverified against the record**, and the reason is that the record contains almost none of the event it was built for. ⚠ **Back on the object, a hand census of all seven sexual-severity scales answers the question the automated searches could not** `[#634「两半都在,而它们从来没有被乘在一起」]`. The criterion was fixed before reading: a scale has a *to whom* dimension if its own codes separate two or more categories of the **partner** — not which sex of the actor is punished, not who bears the punishment, not under what circumstance. Applied by hand to the violence scales it says **yes** (the target is in the title, one severity ladder repeated three times). Applied to all seven sexual scales it says **no, seven times**: three are pure severity ladders, two grade which **sex of the actor** is allowed, two grade **who bears the punishment**. ⚠ **But the census found something sharper than an absence.** The *to whom* does exist for sex — one variable records exactly which relatives the incest prohibition extends to, from the nuclear family out through cross-cousins, matrilineal and patrilineal lines — **it is simply never crossed with severity.** For violence the same ethnographic effort asked *how bad is it, to each kind of victim*; for sex it asked *how bad is it* in one column and *which relatives does the rule cover* in another, and the two overlap on only **25 societies**. **The halves are both there. Nobody multiplied them.** ⚠ **Trying to multiply them anyway ended in two independent deaths, and the second one is the interesting one** `[#635「我预注册的是样本量的地板,而杀死它的是变量本身没有方差」]`. The first was settled before any computation: the variable recording how far the incest prohibition extends is a **nominal typology** — matrilineal is not *further* than cross-cousin — so correlating it with severity would repeat exactly the error this project already killed once, when a variable whose codes were *circumstances* was read as a permissiveness scale. The defensible retreat is a binary: does the prohibition reach beyond the nuclear family at all? **And there the second death was waiting: of the 17 societies with both a scope code and an orderable severity code, 16 extend the prohibition and 1 does not.** The comparison has no comparison group. ⚠ **The stop rule written beforehand guarded the wrong quantity** — it required a sample of at least 15, and the sample is 17. What made the question unanswerable was not how many societies there were but that the predictor is very nearly constant, and a floor on n does not see that at all. There is a balanced contrast hiding in the same 17 — bilateral extension against unilineal, 7 against 8 — but it answers a different question, and choosing it now, after seeing that the pre-registered one is dead, is the move this project exists to refuse. ⚠ **The next test stopped before it began, and counting why produced something more useful than the test would have** `[#636「同时出现的,只是同一批人在同一篇论文里编的那几个变量」]`. The plan was to cross-check the target-graded finding using a fifth harm-side scale — how harshly a society judges rape — and the joint coverage with the three violence tiers is **21, 19 and 14 societies**, all below the pre-registered floor of 30, so all three cells were recorded as undecidable and nothing was computed.
